L45: ## 1 Executive Summary
L46: APPEARS THIS WAY ON ORIGINAL
L47: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L48: Pralsetinib (also known as BLU-667) is an orally bioavailable kinase inhibitor that targets the rearranged during transfection (RET) receptor tyrosine kinase, as well as several other human kinases as described in Section 5 (Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology) of this review. Gene rearrangements (fusions) in RET have been identified as oncogenic drivers in non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC).
L49: Pralsetinib is a new molecular entity and is not currently approved in the United States or any other part of the world. The proposed dosing regimen for pralsetinib is 400 mg orally once daily on an empty stomach on a continuous schedule. The indication proposed by Blueprint Medicines Corporation (Blueprint) for pralsetinib is “treatment of patients with rearranged during transfection (RET)-positive locally advanced or metastatic non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) as detected by an FDA approved test”.
L50: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L51: In the opinion of the review team, the submitted evidence meets the statutory evidentiary standard for accelerated approval. The recommendation for accelerated approval according to 21 CFR 314.510 Subpart H is based on results from two cohorts of patients with RET fusion-positive NSCLC who received pralsetinib 400 mg once daily in a multicenter, non-randomized, multi-cohort clinical trial in patients with thyroid cancer, NSCLC, and other advanced solid tumors (Study BLU-667-1101; ARROW). The confirmed overall response rates (ORR) per Blinded Independent Review Committee (BIRC) in treatment-naïve patients with metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC (n=27) was 70% (95% CI 50, 86) with a median duration of response (DOR) of 9.0 months (95% CI 6.3, not estimable [NE]); 58% of responders had a DOR \ge6 months. In 87 patients with RET fusion-positive NSCLC previously treated with platinum-based chemotherapy, the confirmed ORR was 57% (95% CI 46, 68) with median DOR not estimable (95% CI 15.2, NE); 80% of responders had a DOR \ge6 months. The observed results are judged to be clinically meaningful when considering the intended patient population, patients with metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC, and available therapy.
L52: For NSCLC, ORR may be considered an endpoint reasonably likely to predict clinical benefit when the treatment effect size is large and the responses are durable (Guidance for Industry: Clinical Trial Endpoints for the Approval of Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer Drugs and Biologics). Based on these findings, FDA expects that pralsetinib will have, as described in section 505(d) of the Act, “the effect it purports or is represented to have under the conditions of use prescribed, recommended, or suggested in the labeling or proposed labeling thereof.”
L53: APPEARS THIS WAY ON ORIGINAL
L54: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
L55: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L56: Metastatic non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) is a fatal, incurable disease, with a 5-year survival rate of &lt;10%. The incidence of *RET* gene fusions in metastatic NSCLC is 1-2%. *RET* fusions are most often found in adenocarcinoma histology, and similar to some other subpopulations of patients with targetable genomic tumor aberrations (i.e., EGFR mutation, ALK rearrangement), patients with *RET* fusion-positive NSCLC tend to be nonsmokers and younger than the general population of patients with NSCLC. Selpercatinib, another *RET* tyrosine kinase inhibitor, was granted accelerated approval by the FDA for the treatment of patients with metastatic *RET* fusion-positive NSCLC in May 2020. There is no therapy with regular approval specifically for the treatment of metastatic *RET* fusion-positive NSCLC. Treatment options for patients with *RET* fusion-positive NSCLC include regimens used in an unselected population of patients with NSCLC (i.e., chemotherapy and/or anti-PD-(L)1 antibody).
L57: Pralsetinib is an inhibitor of the *RET* receptor tyrosine kinase. The proposed dosing regimen is 400 mg orally once daily on an empty stomach. Blueprint's proposed indication for pralsetinib is "treatment of patients with rearranged during transfection (*RET*)-positive locally advanced or metastatic non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) as detected by an FDA-approved test."
L58: The primary efficacy data supporting this NDA are from a multicenter, single-arm, open-label, first-in-human, dose escalation and multicohort expansion study in patients with thyroid cancer, NSCLC, and other advanced solid tumors (ARROW). The primary analysis population for this review comprises 114 patients with metastatic *RET* fusion-positive NSCLC treated with pralsetinib 400 mg once daily in ARROW, including 27 treatment-naïve patients and 87 patients who received previous treatment with platinum-based chemotherapy. The confirmed overall response rate (ORR) assessed by blinded independent central review (BICR) in treatment-naïve patients was 70% (95% CI: 50, 86) and the median duration of response was 9.0 months (95% CI: 6.3, not estimable [NE]), with 58% of the 19 responders having observed DOR of ≥ 6 months. In the previously platinum-treated population, the confirmed ORR per BICR was 57% (95% CI: 46, 68) and the median duration of response was not estimable (95% CI: 15.2, NE), with 80% of the 50 responders having observed DOR of ≥ 6 months. These response rates, coupled with the durability of responses observed, are consistent with a clinically meaningful benefit when considering the intended patient population.
L59: Assessment of the anti-tumor activity of pralsetinib in the CNS was specified in the statistical analysis plan as a secondary endpoint. Among the
L60: 87 patients with RET fusion-positive NSCLC previously treated with platinum-based chemotherapy, 8 patients had measurable CNS metastases at baseline as assessed by BICR and had not received radiation therapy to the brain within 2 months prior to study entry. Among these 8 patients, 4 had confirmed IC response (IC-ORR 50%), including two patients with a CNS complete response; 75% of responders had an IC-DOR of \ge6 months. Given the limited number of patients, the point estimate for these results needs to be interpreted with caution; however, the results do support a conclusion that pralsetinib
L61: Pralsetinib appears to have an acceptable safety profile when assessed in the context of a life-threatening disease. The most common (\ge 20%) adverse reactions were fatigue, constipation, musculoskeletal pain, hypertension, diarrhea, cough, pyrexia and edema. The most common laboratory abnormalities (\ge 20%) worsening from baseline included increased AST, decreased hemoglobin, decreased lymphocytes, decreased neutrophils, increased ALT, increased creatinine, increased alkaline phosphatase, decreased calcium, decreased sodium, decreased phosphate, and decreased platelets. Permanent discontinuation of pralsetinib due to adverse reactions occurred in 15% of patients; the most frequent adverse reactions leading to permanent discontinuation were pneumonitis (1.8%), pneumonia (1.8%), and sepsis (1%). Safety issues identified as significant and serious during the NDA review were interstitial lung disease/pneumonitis, hypertension, hepatotoxicity, and hemorrhagic events. These safety concerns are adequately addressed by information in the Warnings and Precautions section and the dose modification recommendations included in product labeling. Risk of impaired wound healing was also included in the Warnings and Precautions section of labeling given the potential for pralsetinib to adversely affect wound healing based on inhibition of the vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) signaling pathway. Based on the potential serious risk of GI perforations/fistulas related to this same mechanism of action, the approval will include a post-marketing requirement (PMR) to further evaluate and characterize this potential risk. There were no significant safety concerns identified during NDA review requiring risk management beyond labeling or warranting consideration for a Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS).
L62: A supplemental premarket application (sPMA) for a companion diagnostic (Oncomine™ Dx Target Test [ODxTT]) was submitted by Life Technologies Corporation (ThermoFisher Scientific) for contemporaneous review with this NDA.
L63: In the opinion of the reviewers, the submitted evidence meets the statutory evidentiary standard for accelerated approval. Pralsetinib has a favorable benefit-risk profile in the indicated population based on the high response rate and durable responses observed in a patient population with a life-threatening disease and an unmet medical need. Given the relatively limited duration of follow-up for both cohorts and the small number of treatment-naïve patients in the primary efficacy analysis population for this application, the current data are considered
L64: adequate to support accelerated approval rather than regular approval. Due to the relatively low incidence of RET fusion alterations in NSCLC, conduct of a randomized trial in this patient population may be difficult. To verify the clinical benefit of pralsetinib in patients with RET fusion-positive NSCLC, additional data will be obtained from the clinical trial which supported this approval, the ARROW study. Blueprint has initiated a randomized, controlled trial of pralsetinib compared to platinum-based chemotherapy with or without pembrolizumab as first-line treatment for patients with metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC (Study BLUE-667-2303; AcceleRET Lung), and submission of data from this trial will be requested as a post-marketing commitment to provide additional information regarding outcomes with pralsetinib in the treatment-naïve patient population.
L65: The review team's regulatory recommendation is to grant pralsetinib accelerated approval for the following indication: "For the treatment of adult patients with metastatic rearranged during transfection (RET) fusion-positive non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) as detected by an FDA approved test."
L66: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	Lung cancer is the leading cause of cancer death in the U.S., with 80 to 85% of all lung cancers identified as non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC).
RET fusions have been identified as an oncogenic molecular driver in NSCLC and have been reported in approximately 1-2% of NSCLC tumors.
The 5-year survival rate for patients with metastatic NSCLC is <10%. There is no randomized trial data available regarding survival specifically for patients with RET fusion-positive NSCLC.
RET fusion-positive NSCLC is a life-threatening disease with poor survival.
RET fusion-positive NSCLC is a rare subset of NSCLC, occurring in approximately 1-2% of NSCLC tumors.
L67: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current Treatment Options	There is no therapy with regular approval specifically for the treatment of metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC. Selpercatinib, another RET tyrosine kinase inhibitor, was granted accelerated approval by the FDA for the treatment of patients with metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC in May 2020.
Treatment options for patients with RET fusion-positive NSCLC include the same regimens as those used for NSCLC without a specific driver mutation.
For treatment-naïve patients this includes platinum-based chemotherapy and/or anti-PD(L)1 antibody. The highest ORRs, ranging from 46% to 58%, have been reported for platinum-based chemotherapy plus pembrolizumab (regardless of histology) and platinum-based chemotherapy plus atezolizumab with or without bevacizumab (for non-squamous NSCLC), with median durations of response of approximately 11 months.
For patients with progression of disease following platinum-based chemotherapy, treatment options include chemotherapy (single agent or docetaxel in combination with ramucirumab) associated with ORR 6-23% with median durations of response in the range of 4 to 9 months or single agent anti-PD(L)1 antibody if not received in the first-line setting, associated with ORR 14-20% with median durations of response in the range of 16 to 17 months.
There is an unmet medical need for patients with treatment-naïve and previously treated metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC. This conclusion is based on the observed response rates, durations of response, and overall survival reported for therapies with regular approval currently used in clinical practice for the treatment of this patient population.
L68: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	The primary efficacy data supporting this NDA are from patients with RET fusion-positive NSCLC enrolled in ARROW, a multicenter, single-arm, open-label, first-in-human, dose escalation and multicohort expansion study in patients with thyroid cancer, NSCLC, and other advanced solid tumors.
Among the 27 treatment-naïve patients, confirmed ORR per BIRC was 70% (95% CI 50, 86) with median DOR of 9.0 months (95% CI 6.3, not estimable [NE]), with 58% of the 19 responders having observed DOR of \ge 6 months.
Among the 87 patients previously treated with platinum-based chemotherapy, confirmed ORR per BIRC was 57% (95% CI 46, 68) with median DOR of not estimable (95% CI 15.2, NE), with 80% of the 50 responders having observed DOR of \ge 6 months.
Among the 87 patients with RET fusion-positive NSCLC previously treated with platinum-based chemotherapy, 8 patients had measurable CNS metastases at baseline as assessed by BICR and had not received radiation therapy to the brain within 2 months prior to study entry. Among these 8 patients, 4 had confirmed IC response (IC-ORR 50%), including two patients with a CNS complete response; 75% of responders had an IC-DOR of \ge 6 months.
Given the relatively limited duration of follow-up for both cohorts and the small number of treatment-naïve patients in the primary efficacy
The submitted evidence meets the statutory evidentiary standard for accelerated approval. The observed ORRs, along with the observed duration of responses, are clinically meaningful when considering the intended patient population.
The magnitude of the ORR in the treatment-naïve population was sufficiently large to overcome the uncertainty related to the small sample size in the context of an accelerated approval.
Assessment of the anti-tumor activity of pralsetinib in the CNS was specified in the statistical analysis plan as a secondary endpoint. Given the limited number of patients included in the exploratory analysis of the anti-tumor activity of pralsetinib in the CNS, the point estimate for these results needs to be interpreted with caution; however, the results do support a conclusion that pralsetinib(b) (4) (b) (4)

L71: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
## Pralsetinib
L72: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and Risk Management	The safety database for this NDA includes 438 patients with RET altered solid tumors who were treated with pralsetinib at the recommended dose in ARROW. The data used to inform Section 6 of labeling and the results presented below are from the 220 patients with metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC who received pralsetinib 400 mg once daily in ARROW. The safety data in this NDA is adequate to assess safety with reference to the overall U.S. target population.
Permanent discontinuation of pralsetinib due to adverse reactions occurred in 15% of patients with NSCLC. The most frequent adverse reactions leading to permanent discontinuation of pralsetinib were pneumonitis (1.8%), pneumonia (1.8%), and sepsis (1%).
While 60% of patients had pralsetinib interrupted for adverse reactions, dose reductions due to adverse events occurred in 36% of patients.
The most common adverse reactions were fatigue, constipation, musculoskeletal pain, hypertension, diarrhea, cough, pyrexia and edema.
Serious adverse reactions occurred in 45% of patients who received pralsetinib. The most common serious adverse reactions (in ≥2% of patients) were pneumonia, sepsis, urinary tract infection, and pyrexia. Fatal adverse reactions occurred in 5% of patients; fatal adverse reactions which occurred in >1 patient included pneumonia (n=3) and
The observed safety profile of pralsetinib is acceptable when assessed in the context of the treatment of this life-threatening disease.
Although pralsetinib can cause severe/serious toxicities, these safety concerns are adequately addressed by information in the Warnings and Precautions and Dosage and Administration sections of product labeling. Pralsetinib will be prescribed by oncologists who know how to monitor, identify, and manage such toxicities. There were no significant safety concerns identified during NDA review requiring risk management beyond labeling or warranting consideration for a Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS).
L73: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
sepsis (n=2).
Safety issues considered significant and serious enough to warrant inclusion in the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI for pralsetinib are interstitial lung disease/pneumonitis, hypertension, hepatotoxicity, and hemorrhagic events.
Risk of impaired wound healing was also included in the Warnings and Precautions section of labeling given the potential for pralsetinib to adversely affect wound healing based on inhibition of the vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) signaling pathway. While there was no clear safety signal in the ARROW study, based on the potential serious risk of GI perforations/fistulas related to this same mechanism of action, the approval will include a post-marketing requirement (PMR) to further evaluate and characterize this potential risk.
In a single arm study such as ARROW, a direct comparison of pralsetinib-associated toxicity observed with currently available therapy (chemotherapy, anti-PD(L)1 antibodies) is not possible.

L82: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L83: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L84: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L85: Lung cancer is the most common cause of cancer death worldwide with an estimated 1.8 million deaths per year and is by far responsible for the largest proportion of total cancer deaths (18.4%) compared to any other type (World Health Organization, 2019). In the US, lung cancer is the leading cause of cancer death, and in 2020, it is estimated that 228,820 (116,300 in men and 112,520 in women) cases of lung and bronchial cancer will be diagnosed, and 135,720 deaths (72,500 in men and 53,220 in women) are estimated to occur because of the disease (Siegel et al, 2020). Survival rates in the US in this patient population are poor. Most patients with NSCLC present with advanced stage unresectable disease (ESMO Guidelines, 2019) and, if untreated, these patients are expected to die within an average of 9.4 months of diagnosis; only 18% of all patients with lung cancer are alive 5 years or more after diagnosis (Campbell et al, 2018; NCCN, 2020). In addition, the disease itself is associated with many symptoms that need to be managed (e.g., cough, dyspnea, weight loss, chest pain, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, bone pain, headaches, anemia, and paraneoplastic syndromes) (Simoff et al, 2013; Spiro et al, 2007). Pneumonia and pneumonitis are commonly seen in lung cancer patients and the underlying disease is a confounding factor for these events.
L86: The 2 main categories of lung cancer are NSCLC (approximately 85% of lung cancers) and small cell lung cancer (Zappa and Mousa, 2016). NSCLC subtypes are adenocarcinoma (most common among men and women in the US), squamous cell carcinoma (25% to 30% of all lung cancer), and large cell carcinoma (5% to 10% of lung cancer) (Zappa and Mousa, 2016). Based on differences in available therapeutic options and other disease characteristics, clinicians also further categorize patients with NSCLC as having either nonsquamous (i.e., adenocarcinoma, large cell carcinoma) or squamous cell carcinoma (Travis et al, 2011). Patients with NSCLC are likely to present with symptoms such as persistent cough, chest pain, wheezing, dyspnea, haemoptysis, and weight loss (ESMO Guidelines, 2019; NCCN, 2020). Diagnosis in the majority of cases is in patients aged 65 years and older.
L87: Over the course of the last 2 decades, the development of genetic testing has resulted in the identification of genetic alterations that play key roles as oncogenic drivers and predictors of responses to therapy in lung cancer. Approximately 75% of lung adenocarcinomas harbor genetic alterations that promote the RTK/RAS/RAF signaling pathway including drivers such as KRAS, EGFR, ALK, ROS1, BRAF, MET, NTRK, and RET, among others (Inamura, 2017; Rosell and Karachaliou, 2016).
L88: RET is an RTK expressed in several neural, neuroendocrine, and genitourinary tissue types that normally requires ligand and co-receptor binding for activation (Mulligan, 2014). Aberrant activation of RET is a critical driver of tumor growth and proliferation across a broad number of solid tumors (Mulligan, 2014). Oncogenic activation of RET can occur by 2 primary mechanisms (Drilon et al, 2018). First, chromosomal rearrangements can produce hybrid proteins that fuse the RET kinase domain with a partner protein that often contains a dimerization domain (Kohno et al, 2012; Lipson et al, 2012; Romei et al, 2016; Takeuchi et al, 2012). Second, point mutations can directly or indirectly activate the kinase (Donis-Keller et al, 1993; Hofstra et al, 1994; Mulligan et al, 1993). Both mechanisms of oncogenic activation result in constitutively active, ligand-independent RET kinase activity and activation of downstream signaling pathways (Drilon et al, 2018).
L89: As the prevalence of tumor genomic sequencing has increased, it has become apparent that oncogenic RET alterations occur in a variety of solid tumors. RET fusions as a result of chromosomal rearrangement have been found in tumors including NSCLC, papillary thyroid cancer, colon, breast, pancreatic, ovary, salivary carcinomas, and inflammatory myofibroblastic tumors, and suggest that RET fusions are oncogenic in many tumor types (Antonescu et al, 2015; Ballerini et al, 2012; Kang et al, 2017; Kohno et al, 2012; Lipson et al, 2012; Medico et al, 2015; Santoro et al, 1992; Stransky et al, 2014; Takeuchi et al, 2012; Wang et al, 2016). Oncogenic RET fusions have been identified in 1% to 2% of NSCLC and the RET fusions are typically found in adenocarcinoma histology (though occasionally squamous) (Lin et al, 2015; Takeuchi, 2019). These RET-fusion positive NSCLC tumors tend to lack mutations in other known driver genes and are commonly found in a younger population of nonsmokers (Jordan et al, 2017; Lin et al, 2015; Lipson et al, 2012; Michels et al, 2016; Stransky et al, 2014; Takeuchi et al, 2012; Tsuta et al, 2014). Activating RET mutations have also been observed at high frequency (90%) of advanced MTC as well as a variety of other solid tumors, including ovarian, colorectal, and breast cancer (Kato et al, 2017; Romei et al, 2018).
L90: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L91: As the Applicant notes, RET gene fusions have been identified in 1 to 2 % of NSCLC and RET fusions are commonly found in adenocarcinoma histology, younger population and nonsmokers. In addition, FDA notes that RET fusions in lung adenocarcinoma have also been found to be associated with poor differentiation, solid subtype, and smaller T stage (\le 3 cm) with N2 disease (Wang R. et al.). The most common RET fusions in lung cancer (&gt;80%) are kinesin family members 5B (KIF5B)-RET followed by CCDC6, NCOA4, TRIM33, CLIP1, and ERG1 (Drilon A et al. and Kohno T et al.). RET fusion is usually mutually exclusive to the mutations or rearrangements of other genes, including EGFR, KRAS, ALK, and ERBB2 (Kohno T et al.).
L92: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L93: As genetic alterations became known, targeted therapies focusing on the specific molecular drivers have been developed to treat patients in a more individualized manner (Rosell and Karachaliou, 2016; Sequist and Neal, 2020). For patients with tumors harboring specific oncogenic drivers such as ALK, EGFR, BRAF, and ROS1, several targeted therapies (including erlotinib, gefitinib, afatinib, dacomitinib, osimertinib, crizotinib, brigatinib, ceritinib, alectinib) have demonstrated significant improvements in outcomes in randomized clinical trials, achieving ORRs of 50% to 83% and median PFS of 8 to 26 months, compared to 16% to 45% and 5 to 8 months for standard platinum-based chemotherapeutic regimens (Camidge et al, 2018; Mok et al, 2009; Peters et al, 2017; Ramalingam et al, 2016; Rosell et al, 2012; Sequist et al, 2013; Shaw et al, 2016; Solomon et al, 2014; Soria et al, 2017; Soria et al, 2018). Accordingly, some of the most widely followed treatment guidelines routinely call for molecular testing and prefer first-line therapy consisting of targeted agents for patients with actionable oncogenic drivers (ESMO Guidelines, 2019; NCCN, 2020).
L94: Yet, despite the strong evidence that RET is an oncogenic driver in 1% to 2% of NSCLC patients, there are no approved therapies that selectively and potently target RET alterations in any of the solid tumors in which RET has been identified. Although many of the MKIs (cabozantinib, vandetanib, sunitinib, sorafenib, alectinib, lenvatinib, nintedanib, ponatinib, regorafenib) have some limited RET inhibitory activity, when these were studied in RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients, they were found to be inferior (ORR of 16% to 47% and median PFS of 2.3 to 7.3 months) to the responses and survival outcomes seen with the selective TKIs in other oncogene-driven NSCLC forms (Drilon et al, 2013; Drilon et al, 2016; Gautschi et al, 2017; Lee et al, 2017; Mazières et al, 2019; Offin et al, 2019; Yoh et al, 2017). Additionally, the studies with these MKIs were often small and thus hard to interpret (ESMO Guidelines, 2019), and none of these targeted agents are specifically approved for RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients.
L95: Due to the lack of available therapies specifically targeting RET, diagnostic testing for RET is not yet standard of care, and RET-fusion positive patients with advanced NSCLC are treated in accordance with guidelines for oncogene driver-negative nonsquamous NSCLC (ESMO Guidelines, 2019; NCCN, 2020). According to these guidelines, recommended first-line therapy in oncogene driver-negative NSCLC consists of cytotoxic platinum doublet chemotherapy with or without immune checkpoint inhibition or bevacizumab, and second line therapy involves the use of a checkpoint inhibitor or cytotoxic chemotherapy. Subsequent therapy for refractory patients is best supportive care or enrollment in a clinical trial.
L96: However, the use of these treatment options in EGFR- and ALK-negative patients with advanced NSCLC and a nonsquamous histology (i.e., in-patient populations similar to the RET-positive patients) does not provide the same level of efficacy typically achieved with targeted therapies for oncogenic drivers. In the first-line treatment setting, intravenous platinum-doublet cytotoxic
L97: chemotherapy of EGFR- and ALK-negative patients with advanced NSCLC is associated with a response rate of only 15% to 31% and a median PFS of 4.5 to 6.5 months for patients with nonsquamous histology (Gandhi et al, 2018; Hellmann et al, 2018; Mok et al, 2019; Sandler et al, 2006; Scagliotti et al, 2008). Addition of intravenous PD-1/PD-L1 inhibitors (pembrolizumab or atezolizumab) to the platinum-based chemotherapy in a population of patients with nonsquamous histology unselected for PD-L1 expression increased the ORR to 46% to 48% with a median PFS of 7.0 to 8.8 months (Gandhi et al, 2018; West et al, 2019), and for patients with squamous histology, pembrolizumab plus platinum-based chemotherapy increased the response rate from 38% to 58% with a median PFS from 4.8 to 6.4 months (Paz-Ares et al, 2018); but these responses are still not as high as the ORRs achieved with driver-targeted therapies in patients with tumors harboring specific oncogenes. Pembrolizumab is also approved as monotherapy (regardless of histology) in the first-line setting, but only for patients with NSCLC expressing PD-L1 levels of 50% or more and who are negative for EGFR, ALK, ROS1, and BRAF V600E; an ORR of 39% to 45% and median PFS of 7.1 to 10.3 months were reported in this population (Mok et al, 2019; Reck et al, 2016). In patients with TPS levels \ge 1%, the survival advantage with pembrolizumab monotherapy was similar to that with platinum-based chemotherapy in ORR (27% for both arms) and PFS (5.4 months for pembrolizumab and 6.5 months for chemotherapy) (Mok et al, 2019). Another first-line treatment option for patients without contraindications is bevacizumab plus platinum-based chemotherapy (with or without atezolizumab); however, although this regimen has demonstrated survival improvements, it is associated with an increased risk of treatment-related bleeding events and death (Sandler et al, 2006; Socinski et al, 2018).
L98: Second-line treatment options for nonsquamous and/or squamous NSCLC are not much better. Single-agent checkpoint inhibition in this setting results in an ORR of 14% to 20% and a PFS of 2.3 to 5.2 months (Borghaei et al, 2015; Brahmer et al, 2015; Fehrenbacher et al, 2018; Herbst et al, 2016). Single-agent treatment with docetaxel produced ORRs of 9% to 14% and a median PFS of 2.8 to 4.2 months (Borghaei et al, 2015; Brahmer et al, 2015; Fehrenbacher et al, 2018; Garon et al, 2014; Hanna et al, 2004). The combination of docetaxel with ramucirumab, gemcitabine, vinorelbine produced ORRs of between 7% and 23% with a median PFS of between 2.8 and 4.5 months (Garon et al, 2014; Takeda et al, 2009; William et al, 2010).
L99: Furthermore, although immune checkpoint inhibition is now recommended for the treatment of NSCLC patients without a known oncogenic driver, the NCCN guidelines state that the presence of an oncogenic driver is a contraindication for treatment with PD-L1 inhibitors (NCCN, 2020). Poor responses have been seen with checkpoint inhibitors in patients with ALK/EGFR/ROS mutations (Gainor et al, 2016; Mazières et al, 2019; Miura and Sunaga, 2018) as well as in RET-fusion positive lung cancers, where an ORR of 0% to 6.3%, a best response of PD in 62% to 72% of patients, and PFS of 2.1 to 3.4 months have been reported (Mazières et al, 2019; Offin et al, 2019; Sabari et al, 2018; Tufman et al, 2018). This low response rate to
L100: immunotherapy in RET-fusion positive patients is seen even in those expressing PD-L1 for a substantial portion of the RET-fusion positive lung cancers, which may be attributed to the overall lower tumor mutation burden in RET-fusion positive lung cancers compared to lung cancers that do not have activation of a dominant driver (Mazières et al, 2019; Offin et al, 2019; Sabari et al, 2018; Tufman et al, 2018).
L101: Depending on the histology, molecular pathology, performance status, age, comorbidities, and patient preferences, the available standard treatment options offer varying degrees of efficacy, but all are associated with serious risks. Infections are common complications associated with cancer treatments, which weaken the immune system. As a result, pulmonary infections frequently develop in lung cancer patients. Myelosuppression is another well-known risk factor for infections and is a common occurrence in patients treated with cytotoxic chemotherapeutic regimens. Cough and dyspnoea are also expected and prominent features of NSCLC patients. The intravenous platinum-doublet chemotherapies (cisplatin or carboplatin combined with pemetrexed) are known to cause severe and dose-limiting nausea, vomiting, myelosuppression (thrombocytopenia, anaemia, and neutropenia), extravasation reactions, nephrotoxicity, ototoxicity, and neurotoxicity in addition to the risk of interstitial pneumonitis, alopecia, severe embryo-fetal toxicity, and sterility (ESMO Guidelines, 2019; NCCN, 2020). Treatment with intravenous immune checkpoint inhibitors are associated with the immune-mediated toxicity (Postow and Wolchok, 2019). These AEs can affect multiple organ systems, causing immune-mediated pneumonitis, colitis, hepatitis, nephritis, epidermal necrolysis, and endocrinopathies that can be life-threatening or fatal (Jingjie et al, 2019; Raju et al, 2018; Yang et al, 2017). As mentioned above, some of the existing MKIs that have some anti-RET activity, while not approved for NSCLC patients, are sometimes used. While many of these TKIs have fewer side-effects compared to platinum-based chemotherapy or immunotherapy, they are still associated with significant toxicity due to off-target effects, particularly liver injury, hypertension, neutropenia and leukopenia, hand-foot syndrome and QT prolongation, pneumonitis, and gastrointestinal events (constipation, diarrhoea, and nausea) requiring early dose interruptions and reductions (Ai et al, 2018; Chaar et al, 2018; Drilon et al, 2018; Dy and Adjei, 2013; Zhou et al, 2015).
L102: Thus, the currently recommended treatment options for advanced NSCLC patients with documented RET-fusions do not offer the efficacy that has been achieved with other NSCLC patients with identified oncogenes (such as EGFR and ALK) and existing non-targeted therapy for these patient are associated with significant toxicity and safety risks. To date, no selective RET-directed targeted therapies have received regulatory approval for treatment of molecularly defined populations of patients with RET-mutant or RET-fusion solid tumors. Given the modest overall clinical benefit of standard therapy in RET-dependent tumors, RET-fusion positive NSCLC remains an unmet need that requires new therapeutic options, ideally that are able to
L103: selectively and potently inhibit RET in vivo while limiting the known safety concerns associated with other TKIs.
L104: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L105: There is currently no FDA approved treatment specifically for patients with RET-fusion positive locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC. The modest clinical benefit and significant toxicity observed with current non-specific treatment options in these patients highlights an area of unmet medical need that requires new therapeutic options that are able to selectively and potently inhibit RET.
L106: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L107: FDA agrees with the Applicant that RET-fusion positive, locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC is a population of unmet medical need.
L108: On May 8, 2020, FDA granted accelerated approval for selpercatinib (RETEVMO™, LOXO Oncology Inc.) for the treatment of adult patients with metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC. Approval was based on the ORR results from a multicenter, open-label, multi-cohort clinical trial (LIBRETTO-001, NCT03157128) in patients whose tumors had RET alterations.
L109: FDA agrees with the Applicant's listing of treatment options for advanced or metastatic NSCLC, irrespective of RET-fusion status. The following tables summarize FDA-approved agents for first- and second-line treatment of patients with advanced or metastatic NSCLC, irrespective of RET-fusion status.

L140: ## 4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L141: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L142: Clinical data from the ongoing, dose-finding and expansion cohort study (BLU-667-1101) were submitted to support this NDA for pralsetinib for the treatment of patients with metastatic RET-fusion positive NSCLC. Three clinical investigator sites, Dr. Justin Gainor, Massachusetts General Hospital, Boston, MA (Site 2603), Dr. Christina Baik, University of Washington, Seattle, WA (Site 2610) and Dr. Gregory Kalemkerian, University of Michigan, Ann Arbor, MI (Site 2611) were selected for inspection, based on the relatively higher number of patients enrolled among the 53 study sites.
L143: The Sponsor, Blueprint Medicines Corp., and the facility responsible for central imaging review, [REDACTED](b)(4) were inspected in [REDACTED](b)(4) respectively, with no findings that required regulatory action (NAI) and, therefore, were not re-inspected for the current NDA.
L144: ## Summary of Inspection Findings:
L145: There were no significant inspectional findings at sites # 2610 and # 2611.
L146: At site # 2603, source documents at the site indicate that in two patients [REDACTED](b)(6) unscheduled tumor imaging was not submitted for central review. In both instances, this did not affect the primary endpoint as neither patient had an objective tumor response per BICR; however, it is noted that per Imaging Review Charter, "any on protocol images acquired at unscheduled visits will be read in the same way as those acquired at scheduled visits".
L147: *Reviewer's comment: An information request was sent to the Applicant to clarify the reason the scans were not submitted to BICR for review. Blueprint stated that earlier in the study, some site staff were not aware that unscheduled scans were required to be submitted to BICR. During data reconciliation activities, the unscheduled scans were submitted on December 16, 2019 to BICR and included in the Day 90 update (data cutoff February 13, 2020).*
L148: In addition, source documents indicated that two patients had died prior to the data lock date but were not reported to the Sponsor and subsequently not included in the data listing with the NDA.
L149: - Patient ID [REDACTED](b)(6) withdrew consent from the study on [REDACTED](b)(6) and died at home on [REDACTED](b)(6); the submitted data listing states that the patient was
L150: last known to be alive on [REDACTED] (b) (6)
L151: **Reviewer's comment:** Prior to study amendment 9 (dated July 3, 2019), patients who discontinued treatment were considered to have completed the study after a 30-day post-treatment follow-up visit or, for patients that had not yet experienced disease progression on study, after disease progression/initiation of new therapy. In addition, given that the patient withdrew from study, no additional data collection was expected from the Sponsor.
L152: - Subject ID [REDACTED] (b) (6) withdrew from study due to disease progression on [REDACTED] (b) (6) and died on [REDACTED] (b) (6) while enrolled on another study. Based on the NDA data listing, the patient was last known to be alive on [REDACTED] (b) (6)
L153: **Reviewer's comment:** Post-progression follow-up was revised in Amendment 9 to specify that patients with documented progression would be followed for survival, which will include subsequent antineoplastic therapy and survival status every 3 months until death, withdrawal of consent, or closure of the study by the Sponsor.
L154: In conclusion, the on-site inspections of the three clinical investigator sites revealed no significant findings related to the data integrity or human subject protection in Study BLU-667-1101. The submitted data listings were verifiable with the reviewed source records, and there were no significant discrepancies identified that would impact on the efficacy and safety findings. Based on the results of these inspections, the data generated from these clinical investigator sites appears to be acceptable and supportive of this NDA for pralsetinib.

L163: ## 4.4. Devices and Companion Diagnostic Issues
L164: Life Technologies Corporation (ThermoFisher Scientific; TFS) submitted sPMAP160045/S019 on April 1, 2020 to CDRH to update the indication of use for the Oncomine™ Dx Target Test (ODxTT) to include the detection of RET fusions in NSCLC to select patients for treatment with pralsetinib.
L165: The Oncomine™ Dx Target Test is a qualitative in vitro diagnostic test that uses targeted high throughput, parallel-sequencing technology to detect single nucleotide variants (SNVs) and deletions in 23 genes from DNA and fusions in ROS1 and RET from RNA isolated from FFPE tumor samples from patients with NSCLC using the Ion PGM™ Dx System.
L166: In Study BLUE-6667-1101, patients were required to have RET-fusion detected by local assessment of tumor tissue and/or blood in order to be eligible. An archival or new tumor tissue sample was required to be available for retrospective central analysis of RET gene status.
L167: Clinical validation of the ODxT Test was performed by retrospectively testing Formalin-Fixed Paraffin-Embedded (FFPE) NSCLC clinical samples obtained from Study BLU-667-1101 and procured samples from commercial vendors. The purpose of the clinical validation study was to provide evidence to demonstrate the effectiveness of ODxTT in reporting RET fusions, to aid in identifying NSCLC patients eligible for treatment with pralsetinib.
L168: The sPMA P160045/S019 is currently under review by the Office of In-Vitro Diagnostic and Radiologic Health (OHT7), CDRH and is expected to be contemporaneously approved with the approval of pralsetinib for the proposed indication.
L169: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L170: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L171: Rearranged during transfection (RET) is a receptor tyrosine kinase that functions in the early development of the human enteric nervous system and genitourinary tract (Mulligan 2014). Activating RET mutations including V804 gatekeeper mutations have been observed in medullary thyroid cancer (MTC) and multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2 (Romei Ciampi et al. 2016); oncogenic RET fusions including KIF5B-RET and CCDC6-RET have also been identified in 1-2% of patients with NSCLC (Lin, Wang et al. 2015). Pralsetinib (BLU-667 or X581238) is a small molecule drug with an established pharmacological class of kinase inhibitor that primarily targets wild-type (WT) RET (IC_{50s} between 0.14 and 0.43 nM) as well as kinase-activating RET mutations (V804L, V804M, and M918T) and the coiled-coiled domain containing 6 (CCDC6)-RET fusion (IC_{50s} between 0.33-0.45 nM) in biochemical activity assays at concentrations below those achievable at the intended clinical pralsetinib dose of 400 mg (free C_{max} of approximately 154 nM based on ~97.1% protein binding). In vitro, pralsetinib also inhibited the enzymatic activity of other human kinases including DDR1, JAK1-2, TRKC, FLT3, TRKA, and PDGFRb at IC_{50} concentrations \le100 nM, all lower than those achieved clinically at the 400 mg dose. Further, despite variability in some of the in vitro assays suggesting a degree of uncertainty of the effects of pralsetinib for these targets, pralsetinib inhibited fibroblast growth factor receptor 1 (FGFR1) in biochemical inhibition assays and vascular endothelial growth factor receptor 2 (VEGFR2) in both biochemical and cellular assays at clinically relevant concentrations. Typical side effects associated with both FGFR and VEGFR were present in both clinical trials and nonclinical studies providing additional support for these kinases as pralsetinib targets.
L172: In vitro incubation of pralsetinib with Ba/F3 cells expressing fusions of KIF5B with RET or RET variants (V804L, V804M, and V804E) or with cancer cell lines endogenously expressing RET mutations (C634W and M918T) or CCDC6-RET resulted in reduced RET phosphorylation and downstream signaling and/or inhibition of viability. In contrast, pralsetinib did not substantially inhibit the viability of unmanipulated, RET-negative Ba/F3 cells. Pralsetinib exhibited in vivo anti-tumor activity in mice bearing subcutaneously (SC)-implanted tumor xenografts bearing RET activating mutations, including the cabozantinib resistant mutation V804L, or patient-derived NSCLC and colorectal cancer (CRC) xenografts expressing KIF5B-RET and CCDC6-RET, respectively. Further, pralsetinib improved survival in mice implanted intracranially with tumor models expressing KIF5B-RET or CCDC6-RET, suggesting that pralsetinib may have activity against RET-dependent CNS metastases.
L173: ## Pralsetinib
L174: Pralsetinib did not inhibit in vitro hERG potassium currents at relevant concentrations or induce QTc prolongation in the 13-week repeat-dose study in cynomolgus monkeys. Single oral administration of \ge 25 mg/kg pralsetinib resulted in increased blood pressure with concomitant decreases in heart rate in male Sprague-Dawley rats. Increased blood pressure is attributed to inhibition of VEGFR2, and hypertension has been seen in patients given pralsetinib.
L175: There were no unique human metabolites detected in vitro in hepatic microsomes or hepatocytes and no major metabolites detected in humans at levels \ge 10\% of radioactivity that might require further toxicological assessment. Following single oral administration, ^{14}\text{C}-pralsetinib-derived radioactivity distributed widely to rat tissues especially the small intestine wall, liver, adrenal gland, uveal tract, and pigmented skin. Pralsetinib crossed the blood-brain barrier in rats and may be present at concentrations expected to inhibit RET at the recommended dose of 400 mg.
L176: The Applicant evaluated the safety of pralsetinib using Sprague-Dawley rats and Cynomolgus monkeys in GLP-compliant repeat-dose toxicology studies of up to 13 weeks duration. There were no mortalities in the 13-week rat and monkey studies at doses up to 20 mg/kg/day (120 mg/\text{m}^2/day) and 10 mg/kg/day (120 mg/\text{m}^2/day), respectively. Exposure increases, particularly AUC increases were generally greater than dose proportional, especially in rats; the greater than dose proportional increase occurred in all reviewed repeat-dose rat studies, including embryo-fetal development studies. Target organs in both species included the bone marrow, with findings of decreased cellularity histopathologically consistent with decreases in red blood cells (RBCs) and mild decreases in white blood cells (WBCs), kidney (decreased weight and tubular basophilia/mineralization), and lung (macrophage aggregation). Additional histologic findings in the 28-day toxicology studies for both species included hemorrhage and necrosis in the heart (although necrosis was associated with sepsis in monkeys). Hyperphosphatemia and tissue mineralization were also common findings in animals and may be related to FGFR inhibition. In rats there was also some evidence of liver toxicity including mild increases in enzymes (ALT/ALP/AST), decreased liver weight, and focal necrosis. In monkeys, the gastrointestinal (GI) tract was an additional target organ with intussusception in both studies as well as extensive GI inflammation and/or ulceration leading to bacterial sepsis and death in the 28-day study at the dose levels \ge 15 mg/kg (\ge 1.1 times the clinical AUC of 43900 ng*hr/mL). Monkeys also had additional lung findings of pleural fibrosis, and bronchioalveolar hyperplasia/metaplasia in the 28-day study at the 15 mg/kg dose level. Consistent with nonclinical findings, anemia, decreased WBCs, hyperphosphatemia, and increased AST/ALT have been seen clinically and the GAVRETO label includes a warning for pneumonitis.
L177: Bone (increased physeal thickness in the sternum and femur) and teeth (incisor fractures and histologic dentin matrix alteration, ameloblast/odontoblast degeneration, mixed cell inflammation, odontoblast necrosis) findings were present in rats at exposures approximately
L178: 0.9 times the clinical AUC of 43900 ng*hr/mL (confirmed by the FDA clinical pharmacology team) at the 400 mg human dose and may be related to VEGFR/FGFR inhibition. Although similar toxicities were not seen in the 13-week monkey study, treatment with 15/7.5 mg/kg pralsetinib in the 28-day study resulted in physeal dysplasia in the femur of monkeys. Recovery was not assessed in the 13-week studies, but increased physeal thickness in the femur and incisor degeneration did not show evidence of complete recovery in the 28-day rat study. The Applicant has not reported drug-related fractures or tooth abnormalities in clinical trials and pralsetinib-induced physeal dysplasia and teeth findings are unlikely to be relevant to human adults with closed growth plates; however, these findings may be relevant in pediatric patients and are included in Section 8.4 of the label.
L179: The Applicant did not conduct carcinogenicity or fertility studies to support the currently proposed indication (NSCLC), and these studies are not needed to support a marketing application for a drug intended to treat patients with advanced cancer. Oral administration of pralsetinib in general toxicology studies resulted in dose-related findings in reproductive organs that may impair male and female fertility. In the 13-week rat toxicology study, treatment with pralsetinib at dose levels \ge 10 mg/kg (approximately 0.9 times the clinical AUC at the 400 mg dose) resulted in degeneration of the corpus luteum in the ovary; cellular debris and reduced sperm in the epididymis; tubular degeneration/atrophy in the testis correlating with small/soft testis; and reduced epididymis and testis weights. These results suggest some potential for reduced fertility in people. Pralsetinib was not mutagenic in an in vitro bacterial reverse mutation (Ames) assay or clastogenic in either the in vitro micronucleus assay in TK6 cells or the in vivo bone marrow micronucleus assay in rats. In addition, pralsetinib did not demonstrate phototoxic potential in an in vitro neutral red uptake phototoxicity assay.
L180: The Applicant conducted a GLP-compliant dose range-finding embryo-fetal development study in Sprague-Dawley rats. Because this study demonstrated clear findings at exposure levels below the clinical exposure at the 400 mg human dose, this study was sufficient to characterize the potential developmental risk. Once daily oral administration of pralsetinib to pregnant rats during the period of organogenesis (gestation Days 6 to 17) resulted in 100% post-implantation loss (early resorptions) at doses \ge 20 mg/kg (approximately 1.5 times the clinical AUC at the 400 mg dose) accompanied by reduced maternal body weight/food consumption and reduced gravid uterine weight. The reduced maternal body weight appeared to be a result of fetal loss rather than maternal toxicity. Post-implantation loss and decreased gravid uterine weight were also observed in one rat at the 10 mg/kg dose level. At pralsetinib doses \ge 5 mg/kg (approximately 0.2 times the clinical AUC at the 400 mg dose) there was an increase in fetal visceral malformations (absent kidney and ureter, absent/narrow uterine horn, malpositioned kidney/testis, retroesophageal aortic arch), skeletal malformations (vertebral anomaly with or without associated rib anomaly, vertebral centra/rib/costal cartilage anomalies), visceral variations [renal papilla(e) not developed and/or distended ureter(s), small kidney/thymus,
L181: major blood vessel variation], and skeletal variations (e.g. reduced ossification). The observed renal malformations/variations were consistent with the role of RET in renal organogenesis. The pharmacology/toxicology team recommends a warning for embryo-fetal toxicity in the label for GAVRETO. Consistent with the recommendations for teratogenic or embryo-lethal but non-genotoxic drugs described in the FDA Guidance for Industry Oncology Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations, and considering the pralsetinib steady-state half-life of 22.2 hours, FDA recommends advising males of reproductive potential to use effective contraception during treatment with GAVRETO and for 1 week after the final dose. Given that the FDA clinical pharmacology team identified pralsetinib to be a CYP3A inducer, which may compromise the efficacy of hormonal contraception, FDA recommends advising females of reproductive potential to use effective non-hormonal contraception during treatment with GAVRETO and for 2 weeks after the final dose to account for the total duration of potential CYP3A induction (i.e. 14 days) after the final dose. The Applicant did not evaluate the presence of pralsetinib in milk; however, because of potential adverse effects of GAVRETO on a breastfeeding child, the review team recommends advising patients not to breastfeed during treatment with GAVRETO and for 1 week after the final dose. There are no outstanding issues from a pharmacology/toxicology perspective that would prevent the approval of GAVRETO for the treatment of adult patients with metastatic RET-fusion positive NSCLC as detected by an FDA approved test.
L182: ## 5.2. Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
L183: ## The Applicant's Position:
L184: There are no referenced NDAs, BLAs, or DMFs related to nonclinical pharmacology or toxicology for pralsetinib.
L185: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L186: ## Primary pharmacology- Completed by FDA
L187: ## A. In Vitro Studies
L188: The Applicant investigated the ability of pralsetinib (BLU-667) to bind to 468 kinases in an in vitro KINOMEsca® screen (Studies #BLU-005-03-s and #BLU-005-04-p). At a concentration of 1000 nM, pralsetinib prevented ligand binding (&gt;90% inhibition) for ~10% of kinases in the panel including RET and the RET mutations M918T, V8044L, and V804M. Binding affinity was subsequently determined for 31 WT kinases. Pralsetinib exhibited the highest binding affinity for RET (K_D = 0.39 nM) and JAK2 (K_D = 0.52 nM) and also bound to FGFR1, FLT1/VEGFR1, FLT3, JAK1, JAK3, TRKA, and TRKC with K_D values \le10 nM. Pralsetinib bound to an additional 17 kinases with K_D values \le160 nM. In an in vitro kinase screen, incubation with 300 nM pralsetinib inhibited the enzymatic activity of 22 out of the 374 human kinases tested by &gt;50% (Study #BPM-0022). Follow-up assays determined that pralsetinib was the most selective for RET (IC_{50}
L189: = 0.14 nM) but also inhibited the enzymatic activity of other human kinases (Table 5) including DDR1, JAK1, TRKC (IC_{50} values &lt;10 nM), FLT3, JAK2, TRKA, and PDGFRb (IC_{50} values \le100 nM) at IC_{50} concentrations lower than those achieved clinically at the 400 mg dose level of pralsetinib (free C_{max} at the 400 mg clinical dose is approximately 154 nM based on ~97.1% protein binding). Pralsetinib also inhibited the kinase activity of PEA1, c-Src, MLK2/MAP3K10, LIMK1, and FGFR1 at IC_{50} values of 144-160 nM.

L193: The Applicant further characterized the in vitro activity of pralsetinib in biochemical activity assays (Study #BPM-0015). At IC_{50} concentrations lower than those achieved clinically at the 400 mg dose level (approximately 154 nM) pralsetinib inhibited the kinase activity of WT RET as well as the V804L, V804M, and M918T kinase-activating RET mutations identified in MTC and the CCDC6-RET oncogenic fusion observed in papillary thyroid cancer and NSCLC. Although several approved multi-kinase inhibitors with RET activity (in particular vandetanib) generally exhibited reduced in vitro activity against the V804L and V804M mutations in the gate-keeper position of the RET kinase domain compared to WT RET, pralsetinib inhibited WT RET, V804L RET, and V804M RET with similar IC_{50} values (Table 6). Pralsetinib also inhibited recombinant
L194: human KDR/VEGFR2 and FGFR1 at clinically relevant concentrations but was ~81-fold and ~26-fold more selective for WT RET than for VEGFR2 and FGFR1, respectively.

L198: The Applicant evaluated the effects of pralsetinib on in vitro RET signaling in cellular assays (Study #BPM-0016). As assessed by immunoblotting, incubation with \ge 10 nM pralsetinib for 90 minutes reduced the phosphorylation of RET (Y1062/Y905) and the downstream signaling protein Shc (Y239/240) in Ba/F3 cells expressing KIF5B-RET (data not shown), the most prevalent RET fusion protein in NSCLC. AlphaLISA demonstrated that pralsetinib inhibited RET autophosphorylation in Ba/F3 cells expressing KIF5B-RET with an IC50 of 5 nM compared to higher IC50 values seen with the approved kinase inhibitors cabozantinib (IC50 = 61.9 nM), vandetanib (IC50 = 833.1 nM), and alectinib (IC50 = 522.3 nM).
L199: To evaluate the effect of pralsetinib on in vitro cell proliferation, investigators incubated Ba/F3 cells expressing KIF5B-RET or KIF5B-RET variants (V804L, V804M, and V804E) isolated from a cabozantinib resistance screen with pralsetinib for 48 hours and then measured cell viability using the CellTiter-Glo Assay. As shown in Table 7, pralsetinib inhibited the in vitro viability of Ba/F3 cells expressing WT KIF5B-RET and all three RET mutations tested at clinically relevant concentrations, but did not substantially inhibit the viability of RET-negative Ba/F3 cells (IC50 = 1873.1 nM; data not shown). The approved kinase inhibitors cabozantinib, vandetanib, and alectinib did not substantially inhibit the proliferation of Ba/F3 cells expressing KIF5B-V804L, KIF5B-V804M, or KIF5B-V804E. The Applicant also evaluated the activity of pralsetinib in cancer cell lines that endogenously express activating RET fusions or mutations (Study #BPM-0017). Pralsetinib inhibited the phosphorylation of RET (Y1062), Shc, and ERK1/2 and decreased mRNA expression of the RET-dependent target genes DUSP6 and SPRY4 in LC2/ad NSCLC cells expressing CCDC6-RET, TT MTC cells expressing RET C634W, and MZ-CRC-1 MTC cells expressing RET M918T (data not shown). As measured by BrdU incorporation, incubation with pralsetinib for 48 hours inhibited the proliferation of LC2/ad, TT, and MZ-CRC-1 cells with IC50 values of 3.7-15.4 nM (Table 7).

L203: The Applicant next evaluated the in vitro activity of pralsetinib against FGFR2, JAK2, and VEGFR2 in cellular assays (Study #BPM-0018). As assessed by ELISA, pralsetinib inhibited FGFR2 phosphorylation in FGFR2-amplified KATOIII human gastric cancer cells with an IC50 value of 201 nM compared to the FGFR inhibitor BGJ-398 (IC50 = 5 nM). Investigators measured JAK2 kinase activity by measuring GM-CSF-induced STAT5 phosphorylation in JAK2-dependent TF-1 human erythroleukemia cells using an AlphaScreen assay. Pralsetinib inhibited GM-CSF-induced STAT5 phosphorylation in TF-1 cells with an IC50 value of 58 nM compared to the JAK inhibitors tofacitinib (IC50 = 33 nM) and ruxolitinib (IC50 = 9 nM). As assessed by ELISA, pralsetinib inhibited VEGF-A-induced VEGFR2 phosphorylation in human umbilical vein endothelial cells (HUVEC) that endogenously express high levels of VEGFR2 with an IC50 value of 70 nM compared to the VEGFR inhibitors cabozantinib (IC50 &lt; 3 nM), vandetanib (IC50 = 63 nM), and sunitinib (IC50 = 6 nM).
L204: ## B. In Vivo Studies
L205: The Applicant investigated the in vivo anti-tumor activity of pralsetinib in female nude mice (9/group) implanted SC with low-passage patient-derived NSCLC CTG-0838 tumor fragments expressing KIF5B-RET (Study #1110-003). When the mean tumor volume was ~215 mm3, mice began receiving vehicle, pralsetinib, or cabozantinib. Oral administration of 3 mg/kg (twice daily), 10 mg/kg (twice daily), 30 mg/kg (twice daily), and 60 mg/kg (once daily) pralsetinib resulted in 62%, 94%, 107%, and 105% tumor growth inhibition (TGI) compared to vehicle, respectively, without inducing significant toxicity based on mortality or body weight loss. Similar in vivo anti-tumor activity occurred following once daily oral administration of 60 mg/kg cabozantinib (TGI = 105%). Further, oral administration of pralsetinib resulted in dose-dependent inhibition of RET and Shc phosphorylation in CTG-0838 xenografts compared to vehicle beginning at 4 hours post-dose (Study #BPM-0020). Greater than 80% inhibition of RET phosphorylation for 12 hours correlated with complete TGI.
L206: **Figure 1: Effect of Pralsetinib on In Vivo Anti-Tumor Activity and RET Signaling in Mice Bearing NSCLC CTG-0838 Patient-derived Xenografts**
L207: The Applicant investigated the in vivo anti-tumor activity of pralsetinib in several subcutaneously-implanted tumor xenograft models expressing activating RET fusions or mutations (Study #CPB-P15-5515, CPB-P16-5665, CPB-P16-5645, and E0400-U1608). When the mean tumor volume was \sim151-239 \text{mm}^3, nude mice began receiving vehicle, pralsetinib, or cabozantinib. Pralsetinib exhibited in vivo anti-tumor activity against Ba/F3-KIF5B-RET, TT MTC (C634W RET), and CR2518 CRC (CCDC6-RET) xenografts at dose levels \ge3 mg/kg twice daily and \ge20 mg/kg once daily (Table 8) without inducing significant toxicity based on mortality or body weight loss. Cabozantinib exhibited similar anti-tumor activity as pralsetinib at the same dose level (60 mg/kg once daily) in the TT and CR2518 xenograft models but was not well tolerated in the Ba/F3-KIF5B-RET and TT xenograft models, resulting in early mortalities. Notably, pralsetinib exhibited in vivo anti-tumor activity at dose levels \ge10 mg/kg twice daily and 20 mg/kg once daily against cabozantinib-resistant Ba/F3-KIF5B-RET (V804L) xenografts.

L211: Further, oral administration of \ge 3 mg/kg twice daily and \ge 20 mg/kg once daily pralsetinib resulted in dose-dependent inhibition of RET (Y1062) and Shc (Y239/240) phosphorylation in Ba/F3-KIF5B-RET and Ba/F3-KIF5B-RET (V804L) xenografts compared to vehicle beginning at 4 hours post-dose (Study #BPM-0020). Across five experiments, the IC50, IC75, and IC90 values for in vivo inhibition of RET phosphorylation were 117 ng/mL, 299 ng/mL, and 769 ng/mL, respectively. Approximately 90% inhibition of RET phosphorylation for 12 hours correlated with complete TGI in these RET-driven Ba/F3 xenograft models.
L212: The Applicant investigated the in vivo anti-tumor activity of pralsetinib in female BALB/c nude mice (8/group) inoculated intracranially in the right frontal lobe of the brain with Ba/F3-KIF5B-RET-luc cells (Study #CPB-P18-21802). Twice daily oral administration of \ge 10 mg/kg pralsetinib inhibited intracranial tumor growth (data not shown) and improved survival compared to vehicle (Figure 2A). Median survival for mice dosed with 0, 3, 10, and 30 mg/kg pralsetinib twice daily was 16.5, 17, 34, and &gt;42 days, respectively. Similarly, twice daily oral administration of \ge 10 mg/kg pralsetinib inhibited intracranial tumor growth (data not shown) and improved survival compared to vehicle (Figure 2B) in the intracranial orthotopic CR2518 (CCDC6-RET) CRC PDX model in female BALB/c nude mice (Study #E0400-U1804). Taken together, these data suggest that pralsetinib may have activity against RET-dependent CNS metastases.

L214: A: Ba/F3-KIF5B-RET-luc; B: CR2518 CRC PDX; BID = twice daily; (Applicant Figure reproduced from Study #CPB-P18-21802 and #E0400-U1804)
L215: ## Secondary Pharmacology
L216: ## The Applicant's Position:
L217: The applicant conducted stand-alone in vitro secondary pharmacology studies with pralsetinib. Pralsetinib was demonstrated to be highly selective against over 450 kinases, as well as against a panel of 55 transmembrane or soluble receptors, ion channels, and monoamine transporters (Report 100023499, 100023915). In this latter study, &gt;50% inhibition of 5-HT2A and Na+ channel site 2 occurred at a 10 µM screening concentration. The clinical significance of this is unknown. There were no apparent 5-HT2A or Na+ channel site 2 effects in GLP-compliant, repeated-dose toxicology studies in rats and monkeys.
L218: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L219: See the Primary Pharmacology section above for FDA's review of in vitro kinase screens investigating pralsetinib binding to 468 kinases (Studies #BLU-005-03-s and #BLU-005-04-p) and enzymatic activity against 374 kinases (Study #BPM-0022). Although pralsetinib was the most selective for RET in these screens, pralsetinib also bound to and/or inhibited other kinases at clinically relevant concentrations.
L220: The Applicant evaluated off-target activity of pralsetinib on 55 receptors, transporters, and enzymes in Study #100023499/100023915 using in vitro binding assays. Incubation with 10 µM pralsetinib resulted in &gt;50% inhibition of binding to 5-HT2A(h) and Na+ channel site 2. Follow-up studies determined IC50 values of 3.6 µM and 3.4 µM for 5-HT2A(h) and Na+ channel site 2, respectively. Given these high concentrations relative to the clinical free concentration, these targets are unlikely to be clinically significant for pralsetinib.
L221: ## Safety Pharmacology
L222: ## The Applicant's Position:
L223: The applicant conducted stand-alone in vitro and in vivo safety pharmacology studies to assess the potential effects of pralsetinib on cardiovascular function.
L224: A stand-alone in vitro safety pharmacology study was conducted to assess the potential for pralsetinib to prolong the QT interval (Report CPB-25-15-010A-0169). The half-maximal inhibitory concentration for the inhibitory effect of pralsetinib on human ether à go go-related gene channel potassium current was 5.18 \mu\text{M} (Hill coefficient = 0.92), suggesting a low potential for QT prolongation. This low level of concern corresponds with negative ECG findings in the GLP-compliant 28-day and 13-week repeated-dose toxicology studies in male and female monkeys.
L225: A stand-alone in vivo safety pharmacology study was conducted in conscious radiotelemetry-instrumented rats, where a single oral pralsetinib dose of either 10 mg/kg/day or 25 mg/kg/day were given orally. In this study a mild increase in blood pressure was observed, with a corresponding decrease in HR. This increase in blood pressure was attributed to VEGFR inhibition (Report [REDACTED]-124581, [REDACTED]-124606). The no-observed-effect level in this study was 10 mg/kg (60 \text{ mg}/\text{m}^2).
L226: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L227: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's conclusions. The \text{IC}_{50} value of 5.18 \mu\text{M} (n=2) determined in the non-GLP in vitro hERG channel study (Study #CPB-25-15-010A-0169) suggests minimal risk of QT prolongation through this pathway in humans given pralsetinib. Though the Applicant did not submit a definitive GLP-compliant in vitro study, the available GLP-compliant safety studies have provided sufficient safety data along with this supportive study.
L228: In non-GLP Study # [REDACTED]-124581, male Sprague-Dawley rats (6/group) received single doses of 0, 50, or 200 mg/kg pralsetinib; heart rate, arterial blood pressure (systolic, diastolic, and mean), pulse pressure, and body temperature were measured continuously for at least 2 hours pre-dosing through 24 hours post-dosing. In non-GLP Study # [REDACTED]-124606, male Sprague-Dawley rats (6/group) received single doses of 0, 10, or 25 mg/kg pralsetinib and the same cardiovascular parameters were measured continuously for at least 24 hours pre-dosing through 24 hours post-dosing. Single oral administration of \ge 25 \text{ mg}/\text{kg} pralsetinib resulted in increased mean systolic, diastolic, and mean blood pressure compared to baseline and/or controls with concomitant decreases in heart rate. Single oral administration of 200 mg/kg pralsetinib resulted in decreased mean body temperature compared to baseline and control. Increased blood pressure did not show evidence of recovery during the 24-hour monitoring period at dose levels \ge 50 \text{ mg}/\text{kg}. Single oral administration of 10 mg/kg pralsetinib did not
L229: result in drug-related effects on the cardiovascular parameters tested. ECG parameters were not assessed in these studies, but were included in the repeat-dose toxicology studies in monkeys.
L230: ## 5.4. ADME/PK
L231: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L232: Pralsetinib was well-absorbed based on the high oral bioavailability (~100%) observed in rat, dog, and monkey. Maximum plasma concentrations were reached after ~2 hours. Plasma clearance was low, with a t_{1/2} of ~3.5 hours. The V_{ss} was moderate and was equal to or up to 4-fold higher than the volume of total body water, suggesting extensive distribution of pralsetinib into extravascular tissues. Exposure to pralsetinib after repeated oral dosing was more than dose-proportional in rats and nearly dose-proportional in monkeys, with no clinically relevant sex differences. Pralsetinib was highly bound to plasma proteins in vitro and widely distributed to a majority of tissues in vivo, including the brain. Pralsetinib was metabolized in the liver primarily by CYP3A4 (Phase I) and UGT1A4 (Phase II) and eliminated mainly via the hepatobiliary route and active secretion through gut wall in rats. Pralsetinib has the potential to cause PK DDIs via induction and inhibition of P450 enzymes and drug transporters.
L233: ## Absorption
L234: The absolute oral bioavailability of pralsetinib in rat, dog, and monkey was high, which was consistent with the cellular permeability comparable to that with drugs that exhibit moderate to good oral absorption in human. While pralsetinib was both inhibitor and substrate of P-gp and BCRP, efflux transport in the gut by these transporters does not appear to limit oral bioavailability (Report 19BLUPP1R1).
L235: Following a single oral dose of 10 mg/kg (rats) or 1 mg/kg (dogs and monkeys), bioavailability of pralsetinib was ~100% in rats, dogs, and monkeys (Reports CPB-P15-10033R02, CPB-P15-10204D03, CPB-P15-10204K04). The absorption was rapid with T_{max} of 2.0 hours in monkeys and dogs and 2.7 hours in rats. Mean C_{max} was lowest in monkeys (396 ng/mL) and highest in rats (2345 ng/mL) and mean AUC_{0-last} was lowest in monkeys (2917 h\cdotng/mL), and similar in dogs (16,910 h\cdotng/mL) and rats (18,797 h\cdotng/mL).
L236: Following a single intravenous administration of 1 mg/kg (rats) or 0.5 mg/kg (dogs and monkeys), mean plasma clearance of pralsetinib was low in rats (14.6 mL/min/kg), dogs (2.0 mL/min/kg), and monkeys (6.5 mL/min/kg), corresponding to ~26%, 6.4%, and 15% of the hepatic blood flow, respectively (Reports CPB-P15-10033R02, CPB-P15-10082D01, CPB-P15-10082K01). Mean t_{1/2} was 3.5 hours in rats and dogs and 3.7 hours in monkeys. Mean AUC_{0-last} was 1137 h\cdotng/mL in rats, 1389 h\cdotng/mL in monkeys, and 4184 h\cdotng/mL in dogs. Mean V_{ss} was moderate (ranged from 0.5 L/kg in dogs to 3.3 L/kg in rats), suggesting an extensive distribution to tissues.
L237: ## *FDA Additional Comment on Absorption (Bioavailability):*
L238: Bioavailability did not appear to be calculated in the monkey or dog study reports, but FDA agrees that it is approximately 100% based on AUC values. P-gp and BCRP studies were reviewed by the clinical pharmacology team rather than the nonclinical team; see Section 6 for FDA's assessment of these studies.
L239: ## **Toxicokinetic Data from General Toxicology Studies**
L240: Repeated oral administration of pralsetinib for 7 days (male rats, dogs, and monkeys) and 28 days and 13 weeks (male and female rats, monkeys) resulted in exposure to pralsetinib (C_{max} and AUC_{0-24}) that increased more than dose-proportionally in rats and dogs (Reports [b] (4) -124551, [b] (4) -124570, 00124770, and [b] (4) -124558) and nearly to slightly greater than dose-proportionally in monkeys (Reports [b] (4) -124572, [b] (4) -124571, and 00124768). In rats, exposure was similar in both sexes (&lt; 2-fold difference), except in the 28-day study at 10 mg/kg/day on Days 0 and 27 when exposure was ~2-fold higher in females than in males. The observed sex differences are specific to rats and may not manifest in humans. No sex differences in exposure were noted in monkeys. In rats, accumulation of pralsetinib in plasma was minor after 28 days of dosing (&lt; 2-fold) and notable after 13 weeks of dosing (2.32- to 3.27-fold). No accumulation was noted in monkeys.
L241: ## *FDA Additional Comment on Toxicokinetic Data from General Toxicology Studies:*
L242: FDA did not review the 7-day toxicology studies in rats, dogs, or monkeys because they are not relevant for this stage of development. Thus, the Applicant's conclusions from these studies were not verified by the FDA. See Section 5.5.1 General Toxicology for FDA's review of the toxicokinetic data from the 13-week toxicology studies.
L243: ## **Toxicokinetic Data from Embryofetal Toxicology Study**
L244: Following repeated oral administration of pralsetinib to pregnant rats, the increase in maternal exposure (C_{max} and AUC_{0-24}) was greater than dose-proportional on Gestation Days 6 and 17 (Report 00124766). Accumulation was minor (&lt; 2-fold) except for 5 mg/kg/day dose (2.5-fold).
L245: ## **Distribution**
L246: In vitro, pralsetinib was highly bound (\ge 95\%) to plasma proteins in mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human plasma (Report 1905093), with unbound fraction in human plasma at 2.9%. Pralsetinib did not show preferential distribution to red blood cells in all nonclinical species, as well as in humans and was stable in human plasma and blood. Together, these data suggest that plasma is an appropriate matrix for measuring systemic exposure of pralsetinib (Report CPB-P15-10118).
L247: Pralsetinib distributed widely to majority of tissues in rats, including the brain (Report Key 1598). Although pralsetinib showed potential to bind to melanin, no specific toxic effects in
L248: melanin-containing tissues were observed in the GLP-compliant repeated-dose toxicology studies in rats and monkeys.
L249: There was a high unbound concentration of pralsetinib in the striatal interstitial fluid of rats, suggesting a potential for high brain penetration of pralsetinib (Report Key 1598). The unbound concentration of pralsetinib in the brain at the therapeutic doses in humans is predicted to be high enough to inhibit RET protein expressed by metastatic NSCLC cells in the brain.
L250: ## ***FDA Additional Comment on Brain Distribution:***
L251: FDA agrees with the Applicant's conclusion that pralsetinib distributed to the rat brain in Study #1598, but also points out that ^{14}\text{C}-pralsetinib-derived radioactivity was below the lower limit of quantitation (BQL) following single oral administration of 30 mg/kg ^{14}\text{C}-pralsetinib to rats in Study #00124834. Overall FDA agrees that pralsetinib crosses the blood-brain barrier in rats and may be present at concentrations expected to inhibit RET.
L252: ## **Metabolism**
L253: The in vitro metabolite profile of pralsetinib was generally comparable across mouse, rat, dog, monkey, and human (Report CPB-P15-10033). In vivo, all human metabolites were present in either rats and/or monkeys (Reports BLU-R5482AM1, BLU-R9705).
L254: In vitro, pralsetinib metabolism in human primary hepatocytes and liver microsomes is mainly mediated by CYP3A4 (Phase I) and UGT1A4 (Phase II) (Report BLU-R9667). The proposed metabolic pathway in vitro and in vivo includes oxidation/hydroxylation of the pralsetinib pyrazolpyridine ring system (M549a and M449c), oxidation of cyclohexyl moiety (M549c), direct N-glucuronidation (M709), and glutathione conjugation with or without defluorination (Reports BLU-R9667, BLU-R5482AM1, BLU-R9705). Metabolite M709 generated from direct N-glucuronidation of pralsetinib appeared as the major metabolite observed in human primary hepatocytes.
L255: ## ***FDA Additional Comment on Metabolism:***
L256: Human metabolism, including CYP3A4 and UGT1A4 metabolism studies, was reviewed in detail by FDA's clinical pharmacology team; see Section 6 for FDA's assessment of these studies. Metabolites M449c and M549c were not detected in nonclinical metabolic studies #BLU-R9667, BLU-R5482AM1, or BLU-R9705; however, M549a,b,d,e,f,g,h were oxidative metabolites detected in these studies. The role of CYP3A4 and UGT1A4 were not specifically discussed in Study #BLU-R9667.
L257: ## **Excretion**
L258: Following oral administration of pralsetinib to rats or monkeys, pralsetinib was excreted mainly as the unchanged parent compound via the hepatobiliary route with a minor amount eliminated by an active intestinal epithelial excretion (Reports BLU-R5482AM1, 00124835). The
L259: majority of the dose was excreted within 48 hours. Urinary elimination represented only a minor elimination pathway.
L260: ## Pharmacokinetic Drug Interactions
L261: Based on in vitro data, pralsetinib metabolism is mainly mediated by CYP3A4 (Phase I) and UGT1A4 (Phase II). Concomitant treatment with drugs that are inhibitors or inducers of CYP3A4 enzyme may alter pralsetinib plasma exposure (Reports BLU-R9696 and BLU-R5500).
L262: Pralsetinib was a direct inhibitor of CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4/5 and a time-dependent inhibitor of CYP3A4/5. Pralsetinib is also an inducer of CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4/5. Therefore, pralsetinib has a potential to affect the plasma exposures of concomitant medications that are substrates of CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4/5 at clinically relevant concentrations.
L263: In vitro, pralsetinib is a dual P-gp/BCRP substrate. Therefore, P-gp and BCRP inhibitors may decrease the gastrointestinal secretion of pralsetinib and potentially increase its plasma concentration. Pralsetinib is an inhibitor of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, MATE1, and MATE2-K and may alter the PK of drugs that are substrates of these transporters at clinically relevant concentrations.
L264: ## *FDA Additional Comment on Pharmacokinetic Drug Interactions:*
L265: Pharmacokinetic drug interaction studies were reviewed by the clinical pharmacology team as part of their assessment, so these studies were not reviewed by the nonclinical team. See Section 6 for FDA's assessment of these studies.
L266: ## The FDA's Assessment (all data presented by FDA):
L267: FDA generally agreed with the Applicant's summaries of the nonclinical ADME/PK data; however, see the FDA additional comments below some subheadings. All of the in vitro PK drug interaction studies (e.g. cytochrome P450/UGT1A4 metabolism and transporter studies) were reviewed by the clinical pharmacology team as part of their assessment, so these studies were not reviewed by the nonclinical team. See Section 6 for FDA's assessment of these studies.

Determination of X581238 Partitioning Between Red Blood Cells and Plasma in Human, Rat, Mouse, Dog and Monkey Blood / CPB-P15-10118	
Following 60 minutes of incubation with 5 µM pralsetinib, whole blood-to-plasma ratios for humans, rats, mice, dogs, and monkeys were 0.49, 0.58, 0.64, 0.53, and 0.63, respectively. Red blood cell-to-plasma ratios in humans, rats, mice, dogs, and monkeys were 0.07, 0.2, 0.41, 0.06, and 0.35, respectively, indicating that pralsetinib does not preferentially partition into red blood cells in vitro in humans or animals.

Evaluation of Stability of X581238 in Human Plasma / CPB-P15-10307	
Pralsetinib (2 µM) was stable in human plasma throughout a 1 hour incubation period at 37°C. The half-life for pralsetinib in human plasma was >120 minutes compared to <5 minutes for the positive control procain.

A Tissue Distribution Study of [^{14}\text{C}]-BLU-667 Following Oral (Gavage) Administration to Rats / 00124834	Following single oral administration of 30 mg/kg (100 \mu\text{Ci/kg}) ^{14}\text{C}-pralsetinib, ^{14}\text{C}-pralsetinib-derived radioactivity was detected in whole blood and plasma for 0.25-48 hours post-dose.
