L438: ## 6.2. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
L439: ## 6.2.1. Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
L440: ## Data:
L441: Pharmacokinetics
Absorption	At the recommended dose of 400 mg QD in patients with NSCLC, the median T_{max} of pralsetinib was 4 hours after single-dose administration and at steady state (Study BLU-667-1101).
Distribution	Human plasma protein binding: 97.1% (Report 1905093). At the recommended dose of 400 mg QD in patients with NSCLC, the geometric mean V_z/F of pralsetinib was 228 L after single-dose administration and 268 L at steady state (Study BLU-667-1101).
Metabolism and Elimination	Pralsetinib metabolism is mainly mediated by CYP3A4 and, to a lesser extent, by CYP2D6 and CYP1A2 (Phase I) and UGT1A4 (Phase II) (Report BLU-R9696, BLU R5500). Unchanged pralsetinib is the predominant component in plasma, urine, and feces, while its metabolites from oxidation and glucuronidation are detected in small to trace amounts (~5%) (Study BLU-667-0103). Excretion in feces (72.5%) is the major elimination pathway for pralsetinib, potentially via hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal secretion (Section 5.4). The mean elimination t_{1/2} of 14.7 hours after single-dose
L442: administration and 22.2 hours at steady state (400 mg QD) supported the QD dosing regimen (Study BLU-667-1101). The geometric mean CL/F was 9.10 L/h after administration of 400 mg QD at steady state.
Steady State	Based on the mean elimination t_{1/2}, steady state exposures of pralsetinib are estimated to be reached after 3 to 5 days of treatment.
Accumulation	At steady state, pralsetinib exhibits minor accumulation (< 2-fold) compared with single-dose administration (Study BLU-667-1101).
Dose Proportionality	Dose-dependent increases in systemic exposure were observed over the dose range of 60 to 600 mg in patients with NSCLC (Study BLU-667-1101), but dose proportionality could not be concluded due to low numbers of patients receiving doses other than 400 mg (PK Report BLUE201904). In healthy subjects, dose proportionality was concluded between single doses of 200 and 400 mg (PK Report BLUE202004).
Interindividual Variability	Approximately 50% to 70% for C_{max} and AUC parameters (Study BLU-667-1101).
Healthy Subjects Versus Patients	A population PK analysis estimated that the bioavailability of pralsetinib was 43% higher in patients with NSCLC than in healthy subjects (PK Report BLUE201906). This higher exposure in patients with NSCLC could be due to differences between the populations analyzed in terms of fasting status. Food has a significant effect on pralsetinib exposure. The healthy subjects fasted for 10 hours before and up to 4 hours after pralsetinib administration, whereas the patients with NSCLC fasted for 2 hours before and 1 hour after pralsetinib administration.
Effect of Intrinsic Factors
Age, Sex, Race, and Body Size Descriptors	Based on population PK analysis, age, sex, race, and body size descriptors (body weight, lean body weight, BSA) have no clinically relevant effect on the PK of pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906).
Hepatic Impairment	Based on population PK analysis, mild hepatic impairment (total bilirubin \le ULN and AST > ULN or total bilirubin > 1 to 1.5 times ULN and any AST) have no statistically significant effect on the PK of pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906).
Renal Impairment	Based on population PK analysis, mild to moderate (creatinine clearance 30 to 89 mL/min estimated by Cockcroft-Gault) renal
L443: impairment have no statistically significant effect on the PK of pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906).
Effect of Extrinsic Factors
Food-drug Interactions	Food has a significant effect on pralsetinib exposure (> 2-fold increase in exposure following a high-fat meal, compared with fasted conditions) (Study BLU-667-0101).
Drug-drug Interactions	
The oxidative metabolism of pralsetinib is predominantly mediated by CYP3A4. Therefore, concomitant treatment with drugs that are CYP3A inhibitors or inducers can increase (with inhibitors) or decrease (with inducers) the exposure to pralsetinib, as confirmed by a clinical study with concomitant administration of pralsetinib with itraconazole (a strong CYP3A inhibitor, yielding a 3.5-fold increase in pralsetinib AUC) or rifampin (a strong CYP3A inducer, yielding a 68% decrease in pralsetinib AUC) (Study BLU-667-0104). Population PK analysis predicted that weak CYP3A4 inhibitor use has no relevant effect on pralsetinib AUC, and that CYP3A4 weak inducer use has a 26% decrease in pralsetinib AUC, which was not clinically relevant (PK Report BLUE201906).
Pralsetinib is a dual P-gp/BCRP substrate (Report 19BLUPP1R1). P-gp or BCRP inhibitors may decrease the gastrointestinal secretion of pralsetinib and potentially increase the plasma concentration of pralsetinib.
The aqueous solubility of pralsetinib is pH-dependent. Therefore, gastric pH-elevating agents may impact the plasma exposure of pralsetinib. Based on the results of a clinical DDI study in healthy subjects (Study BLU-667-0105) and the population PK analysis of pralsetinib data including patients with NSCLC (PK Report BLUE201906), the effect of gastric acid-reducing agents on the bioavailability of pralsetinib is not clinically relevant.
L444: Pharmacology
Potential Effect on Cardiac Repolarization	The potential for pralsetinib to prolong the QT interval was assessed in patients with RET-fusion NSCLC, thyroid cancer, and other RET-altered advanced solid tumors who were treated with pralsetinib at a dose of 400 mg QD (Study BLU-667-1101). Treatment with pralsetinib was not associated with evidence of QTc prolongation. The estimated mean change from baseline in QTcF was -0.9 ms (with a 2-sided 90% CI upper bound of 4.0 ms) at the observed mean C_{max} for pralsetinib in the patients analyzed. No effect on HR or cardiac conduction (PR interval and QRS duration) was observed.
Exposure-response analyses
Efficacy Endpoints	Although statistically significant trends were not observed in the E-R analyses of various efficacy endpoints (E-R Report BLUE201905), treatment with pralsetinib at a dose of 400 mg QD demonstrated clinically relevant efficacy in patients with NSCLC (Study BLU-667-1101). Further, compared with lower doses, the mean C_{trough} value observed with 400 mg QD was closer to the predicted brain IC_{90} of pralsetinib for RET inhibition in humans (1514 ng/mL); the geometric mean (range) C_{trough} value with 400 mg QD was 1150 ng/mL (179 to 4280 ng/mL). Initiating pralsetinib treatment at a dose of 400 mg QD was associated with a rapid decline in brain lesion size, and none of the patients treated with this dose developing new CNS metastases during the study.
Safety Endpoints	Increases in pralsetinib exposure appeared to correlate with the time to Grade \ge 3 anemia or pneumonia AEs, and with decreases in hemoglobin and ANC and minor increases in AST and ALT (E-R Report BLUE201905).
L445: ## The Applicant's Position:
L446: The clinical pharmacology of pralsetinib has been well characterized. The data included in the dossier describe the PK properties following single- and multiple-dose administration of pralsetinib in patients with locally advanced and metastatic NSCLC, after single-dose administration of pralsetinib in healthy subjects, and population PK analyses of data from studies in patients with NSCLC and healthy subjects, together with E-R analyses of data from patients with NSCLC. Results from in vitro human biomaterial studies and from in vivo clinical pharmacology studies (food effect, mass-balance, DDI) in healthy subjects, together with results
L447: from the registration-enabling study in patients with NSCLC, were used to describe the ADME properties of pralsetinib in humans and assess intrinsic and extrinsic factors that may have a potential effect on pralsetinib PK. Results from these studies indicate that no dose adjustment is warranted on the basis of age, sex, race, total or lean body weight, BSA, or mild or moderate renal impairment. Although hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal secretion are potentially major routes of elimination for pralsetinib, and hepatic impairment may result in increased plasma concentrations of pralsetinib, markers of hepatic function (ALT, AST, albumin, bilirubin) in patients with mild hepatic impairment had no statistically significant effect on PK parameters.
L448: The increase in pralsetinib exposure observed under fed conditions can be clinically relevant. Therefore, patients should be instructed to take pralsetinib at least 1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal. Because concomitant treatment with a strong CYP3A inhibitor or a strong CYP3A inducer affected the exposure to pralsetinib, the concomitant use of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A4 inhibitors or strong CYP3A4 inducers should be avoided. If coadministration with a strong CYP3A inhibitor cannot be avoided, the pralsetinib dose should be reduced. No dose adjustment is warranted when pralsetinib is coadministered with gastric acid-reducing drugs.
L449: Pralsetinib has no clinically relevant or statistically significant effect on the QT interval. Exposure-response analyses in patients with NSCLC revealed no relevant or consistent relationships between increasing pralsetinib exposure and efficacy or safety endpoints, likely due to the wide range of pralsetinib exposures observed with the 400 mg QD dose (e.g., as indicated by the range in C_{ave} values from 74.5 to 4287 ng/mL in the population used to analyze E-R relationships for efficacy endpoints). The mean C_{trough} value achieved with the 400 mg QD dose was close to the predicted brain IC_{90} of pralsetinib for RET inhibition in humans (1514 ng/mL), and this dose was associated with rapid declines in brain lesion size and prevention from developing new CNS metastases during the study.
L450: The clinical results of Study BLU-667-1101 demonstrated clinically relevant efficacy and adequate safety of the 400 mg QD dose, and therefore support use of this dose in the target indication.
L451: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L452: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position. FDA notes that dose-dependent increases in systemic exposure could be concluded over the range of 200 mg to 400 mg QD. Refer to Section 6.3.1 for additional details. Also, in the DDI study with itraconazole (a dual P-gp and strong CYP3A inhibitor), the observed increase in pralsetinib AUC_{0-Inf} was 2.5-fold instead of the 3.5-
L453: fold increase stated by the Applicant. Refer to Section 6.3.1 for additional details.
L454: ## 6.2.2. General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
L455: ## 6.2.2.1. General Dosing
L456: ## Data:
L457: The pralsetinib dose and regimen (400 mg QD) was selected on the basis of a Phase 1/2 study that evaluated the efficacy and safety of pralsetinib (Study BLU-667-1101). Based on the MTD and RP2D determined from Phase 1 of the study in patients with RET-fusion NSCLC, thyroid cancer, and other RET-altered advanced solid tumors, and on the efficacy and safety results obtained in Phase 2 of the study, the recommended dose of pralsetinib for the treatment of patients with RET-positive locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC is 400 mg QD. Pralsetinib is taken under fasted conditions, at least 1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal.
L458: The long mean t_{1/2} of pralsetinib of 14.7 hours after single-dose administration and 22.2 hours at steady state with the RP2D of 400 mg QD in patients with NSCLC meant that sufficient exposure was achieved to ensure efficacy could be obtained with the QD dosing regimen.
L459: Covariate analysis of the population PK model for pralsetinib demonstrated that body weight and BSA had no relevant effect on the exposure to pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906), thereby warranting a flat dosing regimen (i.e., no dose adjustment is warranted for variability in patient body weight or BSA).
L460: Administration of pralsetinib with food increased the C_{max} and AUC_{0-\infty} by 104% and 122%, respectively, relative to fasted conditions (Study BLU-667-0101). Because this increase in exposure could be clinically relevant, patients should be instructed to take pralsetinib at least 1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal.
L461: ## The Applicant's Position:
L462: The recommended starting dose of 400 mg QD administered under fasted conditions (at least 1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal) is expected to provide adequate exposure of pralsetinib to ensure efficacy in the treatment of patients with locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC, accompanied by an acceptable safety profile.
L463: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L464: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position on the recommended dosing regimen, i.e., the 400 mg QD dosing regimen administered at least 1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal. The proposed dosing regimen was the maximum tolerated dose (MTD) in the dose finding phase of Study BLU-667-1101 (n=62). The efficacy results from the dose expansion phase of
L465: Study BLU-667-1101 using the proposed dosing regimen, demonstrated that ORR in the *RET* fusion-positive NSCLC population was 57% (95% CI: 46%, 68%; n=87) in patients with prior platinum treatment and 70% (95% CI: 60%, 86%; n=27) in treatment-naïve patients. The exposure-efficacy analysis suggested that no association was evident between pralsetinib exposure and PFS, duration of response (DOR) or best overall response (BOR) in the primary efficacy population (n=149). The exposure-safety analysis suggested that a 400 ng/mL increase in C_{avg} at steady state appeared to correlate with the time to Grade 3+ anemia and pneumonia, and with decreases in hemoglobin and neutrophil counts as well as with minor increases in AST and ALT liver enzymes.
L466: ## 6.2.2.2. Therapeutic Individualization
L467: ## Data:
L468: Based on population PK analysis, age, race, body size, and sex have no clinically relevant effect on the PK of pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906). Although hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal secretion are potentially major routes of elimination for pralsetinib (Section 5.4), and hepatic impairment may result in increased plasma concentrations of pralsetinib, markers of hepatic function (ALT, AST, albumin, bilirubin) in patients with mild hepatic impairment had no statistically significant effect on PK parameters. The renal excretion of pralsetinib is minor (Study BLU-667-0103) and, consistent with this finding, markers of renal function (serum creatinine, creatinine clearance, estimated glomerular filtration rate) in patients with mild to moderate renal impairment had no statistically significant effect on PK parameters.
L469: The oxidative metabolism of pralsetinib is predominantly mediated by CYP3A4, so that concomitant treatment with drugs that are CYP3A inhibitors or inducers can increase (with inhibitors) or decrease (with inducers) the exposure to pralsetinib, as confirmed by a clinical study in healthy subjects with concomitant administration of pralsetinib with itraconazole (a strong CYP3A inhibitor) or rifampin (a strong CYP3A inducer) (Study BLU-667-0104). The population PK analysis of pralsetinib data indicated that patients taking a CYP3A4 weak inducer are predicted to have a 26% decrease in pralsetinib C_{max,ss} and AUC_{0-\tau,ss} (PK Report BLUE201906). Based on the results of a clinical DDI study in healthy subjects (Study BLU-667-0105) and the population PK analysis of pralsetinib data including patients with NSCLC, the effect of gastric acid-reducing agents on the bioavailability of pralsetinib is not clinically relevant.
L470: ## The Applicant's Position:
L471: No therapeutic individualization is recommended on the basis of age, sex, race, total or lean body weight, or BSA. Based on population PK analysis, no dose adjustment is required for patients with mild or moderate renal impairment or for patients with mild hepatic impairment.
L472: Because coadministration of pralsetinib with a strong CYP3A inhibitor or a CYP3A inducer affects pralsetinib plasma concentrations, coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inhibitors or inducers should be avoided. If coadministration of pralsetinib with a strong CYP3A inhibitor cannot be avoided, the starting dose of pralsetinib should be reduced from 400 mg QD to 200 mg QD. No dose adjustment is needed when pralsetinib is co-administered with gastric acid-reducing drugs.
L473: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L474: FDA agrees with the Applicant's position that no dosage adjustment for pralsetinib is needed in patients with mild or moderate renal impairment, patients with mild hepatic impairment, or when pralsetinib is coadministered with acid-reducing agents. FDA agrees with the Applicant's position that coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inhibitors or inducers should be avoided.
L475: FDA recommends that coadministration of pralsetinib with combined (i.e., dual) P-gp and strong CYP3A inhibitors be avoided based on the observed 2.5-fold increase in pralsetinib exposure when pralsetinib was coadministered with itraconazole (Study BLU-667-0104). If this coadministration cannot be avoided, the current pralsetinib dose should be reduced by half. Refer to Section 6.3.2 for additional details.
L476: FDA recommends that coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inducers be avoided based on the observed 68% decrease in pralsetinib exposure when pralsetinib was coadministered with rifampin (Study BLU-667-0104). If this coadministration cannot be avoided, the current pralsetinib dose should be doubled starting on Day 7 of coadministration. Refer to Section 6.3.2 for additional details.
L477: ## 6.2.2.3. Outstanding Issues
L478: ## **Data:**
L479: Not applicable.
L480: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L481: None.
L482: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L483: - The following clinical pharmacology studies are outstanding and will be conducted under PMR:A hepatic impairment study in subjects with moderate and severe hepatic impairment compared to subjects with normal hepatic function (PMR).
L484: - A drug-drug interaction (DDI) study to evaluate the effect of repeat doses of a P-gp inhibitor on the PK of pralsetinib (PMR).
- A cocktail DDI study to evaluate the effects of repeat doses of pralsetinib on the PK of CYP3A, 2C8, and 2C9 substrates (PMR).
- A cocktail DDI study to evaluate the effects of repeat doses of pralsetinib on the PK of rosuvastatin as a BCRP and OATP1B1/3 substrate, metformin as a MATE-1/2K substrate, and a P-gp substrate (PMR).
- A DDI study to evaluate the effects of repeat doses of pralsetinib on the PK of combined oral contraceptives. This study is to be performed if the DDI study with the CYP substrate cocktail shows that pralsetinib is a weak CYP3A4/5 inducer.
- The following physiologically-based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) modeling and simulation analyses are outstanding and will be conducted under PMR/PMC:A PBPK analysis to evaluate the effect of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor on the PK of pralsetinib (PMR).
L485: The Applicant should evaluate the changes in pralsetinib PK in the following scenarios:
L486: - Moderate CYP3A only inhibitor, and;
- Dual P-gp and moderate CYP3A inhibitor.
- A PBPK analysis to evaluate the effect of a moderate CYP3A inducer on the PK of pralsetinib (PMC).
L487: ## 6.3. Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
L488: ## 6.3.1. General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
L489: ## Data:
L490: ## Drug Product
L491: Pralsetinib is to be administered orally QD. The commercial formulation is an HPMC capsule, intended to be marketed at a strength of 100 mg. The drug product manufacturing process underwent (b) (4) (b) (4)
L492: ## General ADME Characteristics
L493: **Absorption:** Pralsetinib is classified as a Biopharmaceutics Classification System Class II compound (low solubility, high permeability). Absolute oral bioavailability was not assessed in
L494: ## Pralsetinib
L495: humans. The P_{app} A-to-B of pralsetinib (0.1 to 10 \muM) in C2BBE1 (Caco-2) cells ranged from 8.81 to 21.2 \times 10^{-6} cm/s (Report 19BLUPP1R1), indicating moderate-to-good oral absorption in humans. Across the range of single doses administered to patients with NSCLC (60 to 600 mg), pralsetinib was rapidly absorbed, with a median T_{max} of approximately 2 to 4 hours (Study BLU-667-1101). The oral bioavailability of pralsetinib in healthy subjects was increased when given under fed conditions. Compared with fasted conditions, C_{max} increased by 104% and AUC_{0-\infty} increased by 122% when pralsetinib was administered after a standardized high-fat meal (Study BLU-667-0101).
L496: **Distribution:** Extensive in vitro plasma protein binding of pralsetinib was observed in human plasma (97.1%) (Report 1905093). Pralsetinib did not show preferential partitioning into red blood cells in whole blood; the human blood-to-plasma ratio was 0.6 to 0.7 (Study BLU-667-0103). In patients with NSCLC treated with pralsetinib at a dose of 400 mg QD, the geometric mean V_z/F was relatively large, at 268 L at steady state (Study BLU-667-1101), indicating extensive distribution from plasma into tissues. The unbound brain-to-plasma ratio for pralsetinib was ~0.14 in rats (Report Key 1598), indicating a potential for adequate brain penetration of pralsetinib.
L497: **Metabolism:** In vitro, pralsetinib underwent limited to moderate metabolism in liver microsomes and hepatocytes. Oxidative metabolism is mainly mediated by CYP3A4 (Phase I) and glucuronidation through UGT1A4 (Phase II). After administration of [^{14}C]pralsetinib to healthy subjects, unchanged pralsetinib was the predominant radioactive component in plasma, urine, and feces, while its metabolites from oxidation and glucuronidation were detected in small to trace amounts (~5%) (Study BLU-667-0103). No relevant interconversion between pralsetinib and its (S)-enantiomer occurred (Memo 805-M10868).
L498: **Elimination:** Unchanged pralsetinib accounted for 66.1% and 4.83% of the administered radioactive dose excreted in feces and urine, respectively (Study BLU-667-0103). Based on nonclinical and clinical mass balance studies, hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal secretion in feces are potentially the major elimination pathways for pralsetinib (Section 5.4).
L499: ## Noncompartmental Pharmacokinetic Analysis
L500: In patients with locally advanced and metastatic NSCLC, at the recommended dose of 400 mg QD, the median T_{max} of pralsetinib was 4 hours (Study BLU-667-1101). The mean t_{1/2} of 14.7 hours after single-dose administration and 22.2 hours at steady state supported the QD dosing regimen. Steady state exposures are estimated to be reached after 3 to 5 days of treatment, with minor accumulation (&lt; 2-fold) compared with single-dose administration. The geometric mean CL/F was 9.10 L/h at steady state. The interindividual variability ranged from approximately 50% to 70% for C_{max} and AUC parameters. Dose-dependent increases in systemic
L501: exposure were observed over the dose range of 60 to 600 mg, but dose proportionality could not be concluded; this could be due to low numbers of patients receiving doses other than 400 mg, compared with other doses (PK Report BLUE201904). In healthy subjects, dose proportionality was seen between single doses of 200 and 400 mg (PK Report BLUE202004).
L502: ## Population Pharmacokinetic Analysis
L503: A population PK model was developed for pralsetinib to describe the concentration-time data obtained in studies conducted in healthy subjects and patients with NSCLC, and to identify and quantify covariate effects that describe variability in the PK of pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906). The pralsetinib data were best described by a 1-compartment disposition and linear elimination model, with absorption modeled via 4 or 7 transit compartments, dependent on the capsule manufacturing process used.
L504: The covariates included in the final model were Asian race on V/F, disease status (NSCLC) on F, age on CL/F, concomitant CYP3A4 weak inducer use on F, patients with NSCLC administered Process I capsules on F, and Process I capsules on KTR. Overall, the magnitude of the effect of the tested covariates on pralsetinib AUC and C_{max} was small (&lt; 0.5-fold effect). Taking into consideration the observed interindividual variability in pralsetinib steady state PK in patients receiving 400 mg QD (%CV of 48.0% for CL/F and 46.9% for V/F), the model estimates of changes in pralsetinib PK related to these covariates are not considered to be clinically relevant.
L505: ## Effect of Intrinsic Factors
L506: *Specific Populations:* Based on population PK analysis, age, race, sex, body size descriptors (body weight, lean body weight, BSA), and disease status (healthy subject, patient with NSCLC) have no clinically relevant effect on the PK of pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906). Although covariate analysis of the population PK model predicted statistically significant increases in pralsetinib exposure in patients with NSCLC (43% higher exposure than in healthy subjects), in the elderly (\le 15% increase in exposure with 80 years, compared with 60 years), and with Asian race (7% increase in C_{max} compared with non-Asian races), these covariate effects are expected to have a &lt; 0.5-fold effect on pralsetinib exposure and the clinical impact is considered to be minor in patients with NSCLC.
L507: *Hepatic Impairment:* As hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal secretion are potentially major elimination pathways for pralsetinib (Section 5.4), hepatic impairment may result in increased plasma concentrations of pralsetinib. In the population PK analysis of pralsetinib, markers of hepatic function (ALT, AST, albumin, bilirubin) in patients with mild hepatic impairment had no statistically significant effect on PK parameters (PK Report BLUE201906).
L508: ## Pralsetinib
L509: ***Renal Impairment:*** A clinical mass-balance study in healthy subjects demonstrated that the renal excretion of pralsetinib is minor, with only 6% of the administered dose (^{14}\text{C}pralsetinib) recovered in urine (Study BLU-667-0103). Consistent with this finding, in the population PK analysis of pralsetinib, markers of renal function (serum creatinine, creatinine clearance, estimated glomerular filtration rate) in patients with mild to moderate renal impairment had no statistically significant effect on PK parameters (PK Report BLUE201906).
L510: ## Effect of Extrinsic Factors
L511: ## *Food-drug Interactions*
L512: The exposure to pralsetinib in plasma was increased when administered under fed conditions (Study BLU-667-0101). The C_{\text{max}} and \text{AUC}_{0-\infty} values for pralsetinib were increased by 104% and 122%, respectively, in healthy subjects after a standardized high-fat meal, as compared with results obtained after overnight fasting. In addition, food delayed the absorption of pralsetinib, with a statistically significant median treatment difference for T_{\text{max}} of approximately 6 hours between the administration of pralsetinib under fed and fasted conditions. The mean t_{1/2} values were similar between the 2 treatments.
L513: ## *Drug-drug Interactions - Effect of Other Drugs on Pharmacokinetics of Pralsetinib*
L514: ***CYP3A Inhibitors:*** Pralsetinib Phase I oxidative metabolism is predominantly mediated by CYP3A4, so strong CYP3A inhibitors may affect the plasma exposure of pralsetinib. In a clinical DDI study (Study BLU-667-0104), coadministration of itraconazole (200 mg twice daily on Day 1 followed by 200 mg QD for 13 days), a strong CYP3A inhibitor, with a single 200 mg dose of pralsetinib increased pralsetinib C_{\text{max}} by 1.8-fold and \text{AUC}_{0-\infty} by 3.5-fold, relative to a 200 mg dose of pralsetinib administered alone. In the population PK analysis of pralsetinib, concomitant CYP3A4 inhibitor use was not among the covariates that had a statistically significant effect on PK parameters (PK Report BLUE201906).
L515: ***CYP3A Inducers:*** Pralsetinib Phase I oxidative metabolism is predominantly mediated by CYP3A4, so CYP3A inducers may affect the plasma exposure of pralsetinib. Coadministration of rifampin (600 mg QD for 16 days), a strong CYP3A inducer, with a single 400 mg dose of pralsetinib decreased pralsetinib C_{\text{max}} by 30% and \text{AUC}_{0-\infty} by 68%, relative to a 400 mg dose of pralsetinib administered alone (Study BLU-667-0104). In the population PK analysis of pralsetinib, although concomitant use of weak CYP3A4 inducers was a significant covariate in the final model, yielding a predicted 26% decrease in \text{AUC}_{0-\tau,ss} and C_{\text{max}} of pralsetinib, the clinical impact of this difference was relatively minor in patients with NSCLC.
L516: ***Gastric Acid Reducing Agents:*** The aqueous solubility of pralsetinib is pH-dependent, with
L517: ## Pralsetinib
L518: higher pH resulting in lower solubility. Drugs that elevate the gastric pH (e.g., PPIs, H2RAs, or antacids) may therefore reduce the bioavailability of pralsetinib. Coadministration of esomeprazole (40 mg QD for 6 days), a PPI, with a single 400 mg dose of pralsetinib had little effect on the total clearance of pralsetinib, thereby resulting in only a modest decrease in systemic exposure to pralsetinib. Based on geometric mean ratios, the decreases were 25% for C_{max} and 15% for AUC_{0-\infty} (Study BLU-667-0105). In the population PK analysis of pralsetinib, concomitant use of PPIs, H2RAs, or antacids was not among the covariates that had a statistically significant effect on PK parameters.
L519: **Transporters:** Pralsetinib is a dual P-gp/BCRP substrate (Report 19BLUPP1R1). P-glycoprotein or BCRP inhibitors may decrease the gastrointestinal secretion of pralsetinib and potentially increase the plasma concentration of pralsetinib.
L520: ## *Drug-drug Interactions - Effect of Pralsetinib on Pharmacokinetics of Other Drugs*
L521: **CYP450 Substrates:** Pralsetinib is a direct inhibitor of CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4/5 at clinically relevant concentrations, and is also a time-dependent inhibitor of CYP3A4/5 (Reports 1812081, 1811301, 1910112). Pralsetinib is an inducer of CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4/5 at clinically relevant concentrations.
L522: **Transporter Substrates:** Pralsetinib is an inhibitor of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, MATE1, and MATE2-K at clinically relevant concentrations (Report 19BLUPP1R1).
L523: ## Cardiac Safety
L524: The ability of pralsetinib to prolong the QT interval was assessed in a subset of patients with RET-fusion NSCLC, thyroid cancer, and other RET-altered advanced solid tumors who were treated with pralsetinib at a dose of 400 mg QD (Study BLU-667-1101). Treatment with pralsetinib was not associated with evidence of QTc prolongation. The estimated mean change from baseline in QTcF was -0.9 ms (with a 2-sided 90% CI upper bound of 4.0 ms) at the observed steady state geometric mean C_{max} of 2090 ng/mL for pralsetinib in the subset analyzed. No effect on HR or cardiac conduction (PR interval and QRS duration) was observed.
L525: ## Exposure-response Analyses
L526: Exposure-response analyses in patients with NSCLC revealed no relevant or consistent relationships between increasing pralsetinib exposure and efficacy or safety endpoints (E-R Report BLUE201905). A time-dependent E-R analysis of pralsetinib in patients with NSCLC (Study BLU-667-1101) revealed a trend of increasing pralsetinib exposure being associated with increasing PFS, albeit not statistically significant. Initiating pralsetinib treatment at a dose of
L527: 400 mg QD was associated with a rapid decline in brain lesion size, and none of the patients treated with this dose developing new CNS metastases during the study. Increases in pralsetinib exposure appeared to correlate with the time to Grade \ge 3 anemia or pneumonia AEs, and with decreases in hemoglobin and ANC and minor increases in AST and ALT.
L528: ## The Applicant's Position:
L529: The pralsetinib drug product is administered as 100 mg HPMC capsules. No absolute bioavailability study was conducted, but in vitro data and nonclinical in vivo data indicate that pralsetinib has a high degree of oral bioavailability (refer to Section 5). Rapid in vivo absorption was observed, with a median T_{max} of 4 hours with the 400 mg dose in patients with NSCLC. The oral bioavailability of pralsetinib in healthy subjects was increased 2-fold when given under fed conditions. As this increase can be clinically relevant, patients should take pralsetinib at least 1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal. The mean V_z/F of 268 L at steady state with the 400 mg dose in patients with NSCLC indicated extensive distribution from plasma into tissues. The unbound brain-to-plasma ratio for pralsetinib of \sim 0.14 in rats indicates a potential for adequate brain penetration of pralsetinib.
L530: Pralsetinib oxidative metabolism is mainly mediated by CYP3A4 (Phase I) and UGT1A4 (Phase II). In vivo, unchanged pralsetinib was the predominant radioactive component in plasma, urine, and feces, and hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal secretion are potentially major routes of elimination for pralsetinib (Section 5.4). Metabolites were detected in small to trace amounts (\sim 5\%). Therefore, in accordance with regulatory guidance, no further exploration of the PK of pralsetinib metabolites was necessary.
L531: Based on population PK analysis, no dose adjustment is warranted according to age, race, sex, or body size descriptors. Markers of hepatic function and renal function were also not among the covariates that had a statistically significant effect on PK parameters, indicating that no dose adjustment is warranted for patients with mild hepatic impairment, or with mild or moderate renal impairment. The PK and safety of pralsetinib in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment, or in patients with severe renal impairment or end-stage renal disease, have not been studied and a recommended dose for these patients has not been established.
L532: Because pralsetinib metabolism is mainly mediated by CYP3A4, and concomitant administration with a strong CYP3A inhibitor (itraconazole) or a strong CYP3A inducer (rifampin) affected the exposure to pralsetinib, the concomitant use of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A4 inhibitors or strong CYP3A4 inducers should be avoided. If coadministration of pralsetinib with a strong CYP3A inhibitor cannot be avoided, the starting dose of pralsetinib should be reduced from 400 mg QD to 200 mg QD. No dose adjustment is warranted when pralsetinib is coadministered
L533: with gastric acid-reducing drugs.
L534: Although pralsetinib is a potential substrate for human P-gp and BCRP, absorption does not appear to be limited by efflux transport in nonclinical species (Section 5.4). This could be due to autoinhibition of P-gp- or BCRP-mediated transport because pralsetinib is both an inhibitor and a substrate of P-gp and BCRP.
L535: Treatment with pralsetinib at the recommended starting dose of 400 mg QD was not associated with evidence of QTc prolongation.
L536: Exposure-response analyses in patients with NSCLC revealed no relevant relationships between increasing pralsetinib exposure and efficacy endpoints. Although statistically significant trends were not observed in the E-R analyses of various efficacy endpoints, treatment with pralsetinib at a dose of 400 mg QD demonstrated clinically relevant efficacy (e.g., as indicated by an ORR of 56.8% and a median PFS of 12.7 months). Any potential relationships between pralsetinib exposure and efficacy endpoints may not be apparent due to the wide range of pralsetinib exposures observed with the 400 mg QD dose (e.g., as indicated by the range in C_{ave} values from 74.5 to 4287 ng/mL in the population used to analyze E-R relationships for efficacy endpoints). Compared with lower doses, the mean C_{trough} value observed with 400 mg QD was closer to the predicted brain IC_{90} of pralsetinib for RET inhibition in humans (1514 ng/mL); the geometric mean (range) C_{trough} value with 400 mg QD was 1150 ng/mL (179 to 4280 ng/mL). Initiating pralsetinib treatment at a dose of 400 mg QD was associated with a rapid decline in brain lesion size, and none of the patients treated with this dose developed new CNS metastases during the study. Increases in pralsetinib exposure appeared to correlate with the time to Grade \ge 3 anemia or pneumonia AEs, and with decreases in hemoglobin and ANC and minor increases in AST and ALT. The decreases seen in hemoglobin likely correlate with the Grade \ge 3 anemia AEs and both were related to increasing pralsetinib exposure.
L537: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L538: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position. FDA notes that dose-dependent increases in systemic exposure could be concluded over the range of 200 mg to 400 mg QD. Approximate dose-proportionality is observed at steady state following administration of 200 mg QD and 300 mg QD under fasting conditions. The mean plasma elimination half-life (T_{1/2}) of pralsetinib is 14.7 hours following single doses and 22 hours following multiple doses of pralsetinib. The apparent clearance (CL/F) did not change with repeat-dosing of pralsetinib.
L539: (b) (4)
L540: The manufacturing process for pralsetinib capsules
L541: (b) (4)
L542: Process I
L543: capsules were used in Study BLU-667-1101 dose escalation (Phase I), whereas Process II capsules were administered to 206 patients in the expansion phase (Phase II) of Study BLU-667-1101, and in Study BLU-667-0101 (food effect study). Process III capsules using the commercial
L544: manufacturing process were administered in the expansion phase of Study BLU-667-1101 starting from May 2019, and in Studies BLU-667-0103 (mass balance study), BLU-667-0104 (DDI study with itraconazole and rifampin), and BLU-667-0105 (DDI study with esomeprazole) in healthy subjects. Although Process I capsules (n=57) resulted in a 24% decreased exposure compared to Process II capsules (n=206) based on the PopPK analysis, the proposed dosing regimen is considered acceptable, given that the majority of the safety data from Phase II of Study BLU-667-1101 was established using Process II capsules with a low rate of permanent dose discontinuations (12%) and supported by no notable difference in pralsetinib exposure identified between capsules manufactured by Processes II and III.
L545: Although the food effect study utilized Process II capsules and not the to-be-marketed Process III capsules, any differential effect of food on Process II capsules compared to Process III capsules is not considered clinically relevant as pralsetinib is indicated for administration under fasting conditions.
L546: Pralsetinib is a BCS Class II drug and exhibits low solubility. The absolute bioavailability of pralsetinib has not been studied clinically. The Applicant describes the absolute bioavailability of pralsetinib in a solution form as ~100% in rats, dogs, and monkeys based on Studies CPB-P15-10033R02, CPB-P15-10204D03, CPB-P15-10204K04. The pralsetinib exposure following a single dose administration of 265 mg pralsetinib (4 x 66.25 mg/capsule) to 4 monkeys (Study 124618) resulted in an estimated absolute bioavailability of at least 80% when compared to a single IV dose administration of 0.5 mg/kg to 3 monkeys (Study CPB-P15-10082K01). Of relevance, the capsule formulation consisted of
L547: The Applicant contends that although pralsetinib is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP, absorption does not appear to be limited by efflux transport in nonclinical species. Based on the results from Study 19BLUPP1R1, efflux of pralsetinib was completely inhibited in the presence of Cyclosporin-A or Valspodar in MDR1-MDCK and C2BBE1 cells. Coadministration of pralsetinib 200 mg QD with itraconazole 200 mg QD (a dual P-gp and strong CYP3A inhibitor) in Study BLU-667-0104 increased pralsetinib C_{max} by 84% (90% CI: 50%, 125%) and AUC_{0-Inf} by 251% (90% CI: 185%, 332%). The 2.5-fold increase in pralsetinib exposure cannot be explained solely by strong CYP3A inhibition, given that unchanged pralsetinib was the predominant radioactive component in plasma, urine, and feces in the mass balance study. Of note, Study BLU-667-1101 eligibility criteria excluded patients who were on strong CYP3A inhibitors or combined P-gp and strong CYP3A inhibitors. FDA agrees with the Applicant's recommendation that coadministration with strong CYP3A inhibitors be avoided based on the DDI study results. FDA recommends that patients avoid coadministration of pralsetinib with combined (i.e., dual) P-gp and strong CYP3A inhibitors; if such a coadministration cannot be avoided, reduce the current
L548: pralsetinib dose by half.
L549: FDA recommends that coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inducers be avoided; if such a coadministration cannot be avoided, double the current pralsetinib dosage starting on Day 7 of coadministration. Refer to Section 6.3.2 for additional details.
L550: ## 6.3.2. Clinical Pharmacology Questions
L551: ## 6.3.2.1 Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of effectiveness?
L552: ## Data:
L553: ***Efficacy Endpoints:*** In the E-R primary efficacy population of patients with NSCLC in Study BLU-667-1101, no relationships were observed between increasing pralsetinib exposure and efficacy endpoints, except for PFS in the time-dependent E-R model (E-R Report BLUE201905). This showed that increasing pralsetinib exposure was associated with a minor improvement in PFS, with a hazard ratio of 0.93 (95% CI: 0.81, 1.07, p = 0.30) for every 400 ng/mL increase in pralsetinib C_{ave}, but this result was not statistically significant.
L554: ***Systemic Tumor Kinetics:*** In support of the 400 mg QD dose, time course analyses of systemic tumor kinetics in patients with NSCLC in Study BLU-667-1101 indicated that the overall relative reduction in tumor size appeared to correlate with the starting dose of pralsetinib and the adherence rate. Patients who started on a 400 mg QD dose with an adherence of \ge 90\% had the greatest and most sustained percentage reduction in tumor size over time (E-R Report BLUE201905).
L555: ***CNS Tumor Kinetics:*** Among the 11 patients who had measurable brain lesions at baseline and postbaseline in Study BLU-667-1101, the majority of patients (9 of 11) started on a dose of 400 mg QD and demonstrated a rapid decline in size of brain lesion after 2 months of therapy. The only patient who started on a dose of 60 mg QD showed a 50% increase in brain lesion size by Month 2 of therapy.
L556: A time-dependent Cox proportional hazard model showed that a 400 ng/mL increase in C_{ave} resulted in a decrease in the hazard of CNS PD (hazard ratio: 0.69, 95% CI: 0.38, 1.26, p = 0.23). The lack of statistical significance is likely due to the small number of patients in the subpopulation with matching PK data (n = 10).
L557: ***CNS Metastases:*** Among the patients with NSCLC who had no CNS involvement before entry into Study BLU-667-1101, none of the 77 treated at 400 mg QD developed new CNS involvement during the study (Section 8.1.2). Among the 8 patients without CNS involvement
L558: who received lower pralsetinib starting doses in the efficacy population, 1 patient developed new CNS involvement. This patient was treated at 100 mg QD for the first 5.5 months, then escalated to 300 mg QD before developing new CNS disease at 7.3 months.
L559: **Brain IC_{90} for RET Inhibition:** With doses of \ge 400 mg QD at steady state in patients with NSCLC, the geometric mean C_{\text{trough}} value for pralsetinib was close to the predicted brain IC_{90} for RET inhibition by pralsetinib in humans (1514 ng/mL); the geometric mean (range) C_{\text{trough}} value with the 400 mg QD dose was 1150 ng/mL (179 to 4280 ng/mL) (Study BLU-667-1101). Stochastic simulations using the population PK model for pralsetinib indicated that only the 400 mg QD dose resulted in a high probability (39.8% of patients) of achieving a plasma C_{\text{trough}} above the predicted brain IC_{90} for RET inhibition (1514 ng/mL) at the end of the dosing interval, as compared with lower dose levels (PK Report BLUE201906).
L560: ## The Applicant's Position:
L561: Yes, the clinical pharmacology program does provide supportive evidence for the effectiveness of pralsetinib.
L562: The time-dependent E-R analysis of pralsetinib for the E-R primary efficacy population of Study BLU-667-1101 revealed a trend of increasing pralsetinib exposure being associated with increasing PFS, albeit not statistically significant. Although statistically significant trends were not observed in the E-R analyses of various efficacy endpoints, treatment with pralsetinib at a dose of 400 mg QD demonstrated clinically relevant efficacy, as indicated by the median PFS of 12.7 months (Section 8.1.2). Any potential relationships between pralsetinib exposure and efficacy endpoints may not be apparent due to the wide range of pralsetinib exposures observed with the 400 mg QD dose (e.g., as indicated by the range in C_{\text{ave}} values from 74.5 to 4287 ng/mL in the E-R primary efficacy population used to analyze the E-R relationship for PFS).
L563: Focusing on the efficacy of pralsetinib in patients with CNS metastases, a graphical analysis indicated the importance of 400 mg QD as the starting dose for prevention of CNS metastasis in patients with NSCLC, as compared with lower doses, and the observation that none of the 77 patients treated at 400 mg QD developed new CNS involvement during the study indicated that a starting dose of 400 mg QD is the most appropriate regimen for the treatment and prevention of CNS metastasis in patients with NSCLC. Further, patients who started treatment with the 400 mg QD dose with an adherence of \ge 90\% had the greatest and most sustained percentage reduction in tumor size over time, as compared with lower doses and lower adherence rates. These results concurred with stochastic simulations using the population PK model for pralsetinib, indicating that only a dose of 400 mg QD resulted in a high probability (39.8% of patients) of achieving a C_{\text{trough}} value above the brain IC_{90} of pralsetinib for RET inhibition (1514 ng/mL), as compared with lower dose levels.
L564: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L565: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position.
L566: ## 6.3.2.2 Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient population for which the indication is being sought?
L567: ## Data:
L568: Based on the MTD and RP2D determined from Phase 1 (dose escalation phase) of Study BLU-667-1101 patients with NSCLC, and the efficacy and safety results obtained in Phase 2 (expansion phase) of the study, the recommended dose of pralsetinib for the treatment of patients with RET-positive locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC is 400 mg QD.
L569: The long mean t_{1/2} of pralsetinib of 14.7 hours after single-dose administration and 22.2 hours at steady state with the RP2D of 400 mg QD in patients with NSCLC meant that sufficient exposure was achieved to ensure efficacy could be obtained with the QD dosing regimen. Covariate analysis of the population PK model for pralsetinib demonstrated that body weight and BSA had no relevant effect on the exposure to pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906), thereby warranting a flat dosing regimen (i.e., no dose adjustment is warranted for variability in patient body weight or BSA).
L570: Administration of pralsetinib with food increased the C_{max} and AUC by 104% and 122%, respectively, relative to fasted conditions (Study BLU-667-0101). Because this increase in exposure could be clinically relevant, patients should be instructed to take pralsetinib at least 1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal to mitigate the risk of achieving an unacceptably high exposure and toxicity.
L571: Treatment with pralsetinib at the recommended starting dose of 400 mg QD was not associated with evidence of QTc prolongation.
L572: In the E-R analysis of safety in patients with NSCLC, there were few consistent trends (E-R Report BLUE201905). Increases in pralsetinib exposure appeared to correlate with the time to Grade \ge 3 anemia or pneumonia AEs, and with decreases in hemoglobin and ANC and minor increases in AST and ALT. The decreases observed for hemoglobin likely correlate with the Grade \ge 3 anemia AEs and both were related to increasing pralsetinib exposure. Overall, the results of Study BLU-667-1101 indicated that pralsetinib at a dose of 400 mg QD has an acceptable safety profile in patients with NSCLC. No risks were identified that were not expected with a protein kinase inhibitor being used as an antineoplastic agent in this patient population.
L573: ## The Applicant's Position:
L574: Yes, the proposed dosing regimen is appropriate for the general patient population for which the indication is being sought.
L575: Based on the considerations above for the clinical pharmacology of pralsetinib, the recommended starting dose of 400 mg QD administered under fasted conditions (at least 1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal) is expected to provide adequate exposure of pralsetinib to ensure efficacy in the treatment of patients with locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC.
L576: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L577: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position. The proposed dosing regimen was identified as the MTD in the dose finding phase of Study BLU-667-1101 (n=62). Based on efficacy results from the dose expansion phase of Study BLU-667-1101 using the proposed dosing regimen, the ORR in the *RET* fusion-positive NSCLC population is 57% (95% CI: 46%, 68%; n=87) in patients with prior platinum treatment and 70% (95% CI: 60%, 86%; n=27) in treatment-naïve patients.
L578: ## 6.3.2.3 Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors?
L579: ## Data:
L580: Although covariate analysis of a population PK model predicted that patients with advanced age (80 versus 60 years), or Asian race (versus non-Asian patients), would experience increased pralsetinib exposure (&lt; 0.5-fold), the clinical impact of these covariates is not considered to be relevant in patients with NSCLC (PK Report BLUE201906). The covariates of sex, total or lean body weight, and BSA were not among the covariates that had a statistically significant effect on PK parameters.
L581: As hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal secretion are potentially major routes of elimination for pralsetinib (Section 5.4), hepatic impairment may result in increased plasma concentrations of pralsetinib. However, in the population PK analysis of pralsetinib, markers of hepatic function (ALT, AST, albumin, bilirubin) were not among the covariates that had a statistically significant effect on PK parameters.
L582: A clinical mass-balance study in healthy subjects demonstrated that the renal excretion of pralsetinib is low, with only 6% of the administered dose ([14C]pralsetinib) recovered in urine (Study BLU-667-0103). Consistent with this finding, in the population PK analysis of pralsetinib, markers of renal function (serum creatinine, creatinine clearance, estimated glomerular filtration rate) were not among the covariates that had a statistically significant effect on PK
L583: parameters.
L584: ## The Applicant's Position:
L585: No, an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy for pralsetinib treatment is not required for subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors.
L586: Based on population PK analysis, no dose adjustment is warranted on the basis of age, sex, race, total or lean body weight, or BSA. Further, no dose adjustment is warranted for patients with mild hepatic impairment, or with mild or moderate renal impairment. The PK and safety of pralsetinib in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment, or in patients with severe renal impairment or end-stage renal disease, have not been studied and a recommended dose for these patients has not been established.
L587: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L588: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position. A hepatic impairment study in subjects with moderate and severe hepatic impairment compared to subjects with normal hepatic function is required as a PMR. The PopPK analysis included 18 patients with mild hepatic impairment and suggested no effect of mild hepatic impairment on the exposure of pralsetinib. In the mass balance study, 6% of the pralsetinib radioactive dose was recovered in the urine. Based on the exposure-safety analysis, higher pralsetinib exposure was associated with a higher risk of Grade 3+ anemia and pneumonia. In addition, AST increased in 41% of patients (Grade 3/4 in 6%) and ALT in 29% of patients (Grade 3/4 in 3.4%) in Study BLU-667-1101.
L589: 6.3.2.4 Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and what is the appropriate management strategy?
L590: ## Data:
L591: ## *Food-drug Interaction*
L592: The exposure to pralsetinib in plasma was increased when administered under fed conditions. The C_{max} and AUC_{0-\infty} values for pralsetinib were increased by 104% and 122%, respectively, in healthy subjects after a standardized high-fat meal, as compared with results obtained after overnight fasting (Study BLU-667-0101).
L593: ## *Drug-drug Interactions - Effect of Other Drugs on Pharmacokinetics of Pralsetinib*
L594: Pralsetinib Phase I metabolism is predominantly mediated by CYP3A4. Therefore, CYP3A inhibitors and inducers may affect the plasma exposure of pralsetinib. The aqueous solubility of pralsetinib is pH-dependent, with higher pH resulting in lower solubility. Drugs that elevate the
L595: ## Pralsetinib
L596: gastric pH (e.g., PPIs, H2RAs, or antacids) may therefore reduce the bioavailability of pralsetinib.
L597: **CYP3A Inhibitors:** Coadministration of itraconazole, a strong CYP3A inhibitor, with a single dose of pralsetinib increased pralsetinib C_{max} by 1.8-fold and AUC_{0-\infty} by 3.5-fold, relative to pralsetinib administered alone (Study BLU-667-0104). In the population PK analysis of pralsetinib, concomitant CYP3A4 inhibitor use was not among the covariates that had a statistically significant effect on PK parameters (PK Report BLUE201906).
L598: **CYP3A Inducers:** Coadministration of rifampin (600 mg QD for 16 days), a strong CYP3A inducer, with a single 400 mg dose of pralsetinib decreased pralsetinib C_{max} by 30% and AUC_{0-\infty} by 68%, relative to a 400 mg dose of pralsetinib administered alone (Study BLU-667-0104). In the population PK analysis of pralsetinib, although concomitant use of weak CYP3A4 inducers was a significant covariate in the final model, yielding a predicted 26% decrease in the AUC and C_{max} of pralsetinib, the clinical impact of this covariate was relatively minor in patients with NSCLC.
L599: **Gastric Acid Reducing Agents:** Coadministration of esomeprazole, a PPI, with a single dose of pralsetinib had little effect on the total clearance of pralsetinib, thereby resulting in only a modest decrease in systemic exposure to pralsetinib. Based on geometric mean ratios, the decreases were 25% for C_{max}, and 15% for AUC (Study BLU-667-0105). In the population PK analysis of pralsetinib, concomitant use of PPIs, H2RAs, or antacids was not among the covariates that had a statistically significant effect on PK parameters. Based on these results, the effect of gastric acid-reducing drugs on the bioavailability of pralsetinib is not clinically relevant.
L600: **Transporters:** Pralsetinib is a dual P-gp/BCRP substrate (Report 19BLUPP1R1). P-gp or BCRP inhibitors may decrease the gastrointestinal secretion of pralsetinib and potentially increase the plasma concentration of pralsetinib. However, despite pralsetinib being both an inhibitor and a substrate of P-gp and BCRP, 100% bioavailability of pralsetinib was observed in preclinical species (Section 5.4). Further, dose-dependent increases in pralsetinib exposure were observed over the dose range of 60 to 600 mg in patients with NSCLC, along with a dose-proportional increase in pralsetinib exposure over the dose range of 200 to 400 mg in healthy subjects (Section 6.2.1). Together, these results suggest that the impact of P-gp or BCRP inhibitors on pralsetinib exposure is minimal.
L601: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L602: Yes, there are clinically relevant food-drug and drug-drug interactions for pralsetinib, and appropriate management strategies are proposed.
L603: The potential clinical relevance of the 2-fold increase in exposure when pralsetinib is
L604: administered under fed conditions cannot be disregarded because it could effectively increase pralsetinib exposure at the therapeutic doses either approaching or in excess of that observed at the MTD of 400 mg QD. The safety, efficacy, and PK data for pralsetinib in patients with NSCLC were obtained after treatment under fasted conditions. Therefore, patients should be instructed to take pralsetinib at least 1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal.
L605: Because coadministration of pralsetinib with a strong CYP3A inhibitor increases pralsetinib plasma concentrations, coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inhibitors should be avoided. If coadministration of pralsetinib with a strong CYP3A inhibitor cannot be avoided, the starting dose of pralsetinib should be reduced from 400 mg QD to 200 mg QD. Because coadministration of pralsetinib with a strong CYP3A inducer decreases pralsetinib plasma concentrations, coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inducers should be avoided.
L606: Based on the results of a clinical DDI study and the population PK analysis of pralsetinib including data from patients with NSCLC, the effect of gastric acid-reducing drugs on the bioavailability of pralsetinib is not clinically relevant and no dose adjustment is needed in the presence of gastric acid-reducing drugs.
L607: No management strategies are proposed for coadministration of pralsetinib with P-gp and BCRP inhibitors given that the effect on pralsetinib exposure is predicted to be minimal when these agents are coadministered with pralsetinib. In addition, the majority of CYP3A4 inhibitors and inducers are also P-gp inhibitors and inducers, and hence the management strategy applied to strong CYP3A4 inhibitors and inducers should adequately cover the interactions mediated by P-gp.
L608: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L609: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position regarding food effect, coadministration with gastric acid-reducing drugs and recommendation to avoid coadministration of strong CYP3A inhibitors and inducers.
L610: Coadministration of pralsetinib 200 mg QD with itraconazole 200 mg QD (a dual P-gp and strong CYP3A inhibitor) in Study BLU-667-0104 increased pralsetinib C_{max} by 84% (90% CI: 50%, 125%) and AUC_{0-Inf} by 251% (90% CI: 185%, 332%). The significant increase in pralsetinib exposure cannot be explained solely by strong CYP3A inhibition, given that unchanged pralsetinib was the predominant radioactive component in plasma, urine, and feces, with &lt;5% metabolites from oxidation detected in the mass balance study. FDA recommends that patients avoid coadministration of pralsetinib with combined (dual) P-gp and strong CYP3A inhibitors; if such a coadministration cannot be avoided, reduce the current pralsetinib dose as recommended in Table 11. Of note, Study BLU-667-1101 eligibility criteria excluded patients
L611: who were on strong CYP3A inhibitors or combined P-gp and strong CYP3A inhibitors.
L612: **Table 11. Recommended Dosage Modifications for GAVRETO for Coadministration with Combined Strong CYP3A and P-gp Inhibitors**
L613: Current GAVRETO Dosage	Recommended GAVRETO Dosage
400 mg orally once daily	200 mg orally once daily
300 mg orally once daily	200 mg orally once daily
200 mg orally once daily	100 mg orally once daily
L614: Coadministration of rifampin 600 mg QD (dual P-gp and strong CYP3A inducer) with a single pralsetinib dose of 400 mg in Study BLU-667-0104 reduced pralsetinib C_{max} by 30.1% (90% CI: 19.6%, 39%) and AUC_{0-INF} by 68.2% (90% CI: 63%, 73%). Of note, strong CYP3A inducers are also inducers of P-gp via pregnane X receptor (PXR) activation. FDA recommends that coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inducers be avoided; if the coadministration cannot be avoided, double the current pralsetinib dose starting on Day 7 of coadministration; after the inducer has been discontinued for at least 14 days, resume the pralsetinib dose taken prior to initiating the strong CYP3A inducer. In Study BLU-667-0101, 37% of patients with *RET* fusion-positive NSCLC had brain metastasis that likely require treatment with antiepileptics. Enzyme-inducing antiepileptics often have strong CYP3A induction effects (e.g., carbamazepine, phenytoin). This recommendation ensures that patients with brain metastasis on enzyme-inducing antiepileptics achieve pralsetinib mean C_{trough, ss} levels of the 400 mg QD regimen and close to the predicted brain IC_{90} of pralsetinib for *RET* inhibition in humans (1514 ng/mL).
L615: FDA does not agree with the Applicant's position that the impact of P-gp inhibitors on pralsetinib exposure is minimal as efflux of pralsetinib was completely inhibited in the presence of Cyclosporin-A or Valspodar in MDR1-MDCK and C2BBE1 cells in Study 19BLUPP1R1. In the DDI Study BLU-667-0104, a 2.5-fold increase in pralsetinib AUC_{0-INF} was observed when coadministered with itraconazole (a dual P-gp and strong CYP3A inhibitor) which cannot be explained solely by inhibition of CYP3A as discussed in Sections 6.3.1 and 6.3.2.
L616: Table 12 and 13 below summarize the outstanding clinical DDI studies and PBPK modeling and simulation analyses and their rationales.
L617: **Table 12. Outstanding Dedicated Clinical DDI Studies and Their Rationales.**
L618: DDI Study	Rationale
To evaluate the effect of repeat doses of a P-gp inhibitor on the PK	The DDI Study BLU-667-0104 with itraconazole (a dual P-gp and strong CYP3A inhibitor) resulted in a 2.5-fold increase in pralsetinib AUC_{0-INF}. This magnitude of increase in pralsetinib exposure cannot be explained solely by strong CYP3A inhibition. The purpose of the DDI study with a
L619: of pralsetinib (PMR)	
P-gp inhibitor is to identify the contribution of P-gp inhibition to the exposure increase observed in Study BLU-667-0104, evaluate the magnitude of increase in pralsetinib exposure that is attributable to P-gp inhibition, and allow for appropriate dosage adjustment for pralsetinib when coadministered with strong CYP3A inhibitors or P-gp inhibitors, if warranted.
The information from the DDI study with a P-gp inhibitor is required for inclusion in the PBPK modeling and simulation to evaluate the changes in pralsetinib PK when coadministered with a dual (combined) P-gp and moderate CYP3A inhibitor (see Table 13).

A cocktail DDI study to evaluate the effects of repeat doses of pralsetinib on the PK of CYP3A, 2C8, and 2C9 substrates (PMR)	
Pralsetinib is identified as an inhibitor and inducer of CYP3A4/5 with time-dependent inhibition of CYP3A4/5, and also an inhibitor and inducer of CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 at clinically relevant concentrations. Coadministering pralsetinib with sensitive substrates of CYP3A4/5, CYP2C8, and CYP2C9 may increase or decrease the exposure of these sensitive CYP enzyme substrates which may lead to an increased risk of adverse reactions or reduced efficacy of these sensitive CYP enzyme substrates.

A cocktail DDI study to evaluate the effects of repeat doses of pralsetinib on the PK of rosuvastatin as a BCRP and OATP1B1/3 substrate, metformin as a MATE1/2-K substrate, and a P-gp substrate (PMR)	
Pralsetinib is identified as an inhibitor of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, as well as MATE1, and MATE-2K transporters, at clinically relevant concentrations.
Given that pralsetinib is an inhibitor of both OATP1B1/3 and BCRP, a clinical DDI study to evaluate the effect of pralsetinib on the PK of rosuvastatin is warranted. The treatment modifications of rosuvastatin in 21 patients in Study BLU-667-1101 were assessed; however, the safety analysis may not accurately reflect changes in rosuvastatin exposure due to confounding factors in Study BLU-667-1101 and may not be sufficiently sensitive, as the primary source of evidence, to replace a clinical drug interaction trial. Of note, signs of statin toxicity may be masked by pralsetinib adverse events (i.e., liver enzyme elevations). Dyslipidemics are the most commonly used category of drugs for supportive care in lung cancer; this assessment would provide an anti-hyperlipidemia treatment option with appropriate dosing recommendations when coadministered with pralsetinib.
Serum creatinine elevations (Grade 1-4) were observed in 41% of patients in Study BLU-667-1101. An assessment of the risk of hypoglycemia cannot be made as blood glucose concentrations at steady state compared to baseline were available in only 5 patients
L620: receiving concomitant metformin in Study BLU-667-1101. A clinical DDI study to evaluate the effect of pralsetinib on the PK of metformin, a MATEs substrate, is warranted.
Some P-gp substrates (e.g., digoxin) have narrow therapeutic indices. Specific dose reductions for P-gp substrates are recommended based on the magnitude of increase in the exposure of the P-gp substrate. A clinical DDI study to evaluate the effect of pralsetinib on the PK of a P-gp substrate is warranted.

A DDI study to evaluate the effects of repeat doses of pralsetinib on the PK of combined oral contraceptives	Pralsetinib is teratogenic and intended for use in women with child-bearing potential. Pralsetinib is potentially a CYP3A inducer; efficacy of combined hormonal contraceptives may be compromised. This study is to be performed if the DDI study with the cocktail of CYP substrates shows that pralsetinib is a weak CYP3A4/5 inducer. Of note, combined oral contraceptives are contraindicated with strong and moderate CYP3A inducers.
L621: Table 13. Outstanding PBPK Modeling and Simulation Analyses and Their Rationales.
L622: DDI Study	Rationale
To evaluate the effect of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor on the PK of pralsetinib (PMR)	Pralsetinib is identified as a CYP3A substrate. The DDI study with itraconazole (a dual P-gp and strong CYP3A inhibitor) resulted in a 2.5-fold increase in pralsetinib exposure as compared to pralsetinib administered alone. Coadministering pralsetinib with moderate CYP3A inhibitors may increase pralsetinib exposure which may lead to an increased risk of adverse reactions.
To evaluate the effect of a combined (dual) P-gp and moderate CYP3A inhibitor on the PK of pralsetinib (PMR)	Coadministering pralsetinib with dual P-gp and moderate CYP3A inhibitors may increase pralsetinib exposure which may lead to an increased risk of adverse reactions.
To evaluate the effect of a moderate CYP3A inducer on the PK of pralsetinib (PMC)	The DDI study with rifampin (a strong CYP3A inducer) resulted in a 68% decrease in pralsetinib exposure as compared with pralsetinib administered alone. Pralsetinib exposure decreased by 26% when coadministered with weak CYP3A inducers based on PopPK analysis. Coadministering pralsetinib with moderate CYP3A inducers may
L623: decrease pralsetinib exposure by a clinically relevant magnitude which may lead to decreased efficacy of pralsetinib.

L653: FDA agrees that the studies listed in the table above are pertinent to the evaluation of efficacy and safety of pralsetinib to support the proposed indication.
L654: The primary clinical study to support the evaluation of efficacy and safety for the proposed indication is Study BLU-667-1101 (ARROW). The submission contains an interim Clinical Study Report (CSR 1) dated February 26, 2020, with the data cutoff date of November 18, 2019. CSR 1 presents efficacy results for 132 NSCLC RET-fusion -positive patients who received pralsetinib at a starting dose of 400 mg once daily (QD) by July 11, 2019 [in Group 1 or 2 of the dose expansion (Phase 2) part, or in the dose-finding (Phase 1) part of the study] and safety results for 404 patients who received at least one dose of pralsetinib enrolled in either the dose-finding part (Phase 1) and dose expansion part (Phase 2).
L655: On June 11, 2020, the Applicant submitted a 90-day safety update and a 90-day efficacy update for the ARROW study
L656: The 90-day efficacy update with the data cut-off date of February 13, 2020 contains analysis of the efficacy population of NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD, enrolled as of July 11, 2019 (N=132) and additional efficacy analyses for the population of patients with "RET-altered measurable disease", comprised of 125 patients treated at 400 mg QD. The proposal to limit the efficacy population to include only patients with measurable disease was discussed at the Applicant Orientation Meeting on May 14, 2020. FDA requested that the Applicant submit the pertinent analysis for review and consideration for inclusion in the USPI.
L657: Per FDA's request, the Applicant submitted a revised dataset package (ADaM and SDTM) with the 90-day data update based on the cutoff date of February 13, 2020. The datasets were received on June 18, 2020 under SDN 22.

L659: ## 8 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
L660: ## 8.1. Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L661: Efficacy claims in this application are based on the results from the pivotal Phase 1/2 Study BLU-667-1101. Efficacy results of pralsetinib from this study are provided for the intended population of patients with RET-fusion positive NSCLC dosed on or before 11 July 2019 (data cutoff of 18 November 2019). The focus of this application is on the proposed indication of RET-positive locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC.
L662: ***FDA Reviewer's comment:*** As stated in section 7.1, the initial efficacy population, as agreed upon at the time of the pre-NDA meeting, consisted of efficacy data from 132 patients with RET-altered NSCLC treated at 400 mg QD, enrolled by July 11, 2019. Additional efficacy analyses for the “RET-altered measurable disease population”, comprised of 125 patients treated at 400 mg QD were submitted on June 11, 2020 for inclusion in the USPI. FDA’s primary efficacy is based on the subpopulation of patients with measurable metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC who had received prior platinum-based chemotherapy treated at 400 mg QD, enrolled by July 11, 2019 (N=87) and on the subpopulation of patients with measurable metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC who had not received prior treatment for their metastatic disease (N=27). The efficacy analyses submitted by the Applicant in the following sections represent data from 132 patients with RET fusion-positive NSCLC treated at 400 mg QD, enrolled by July 11, 2019, with measurable and non-measurable disease.
L663: ## 8.1.1. A Phase 1/2 Study of the Highly-Selective RET Inhibitor, Pralsetinib, in Patients with Thyroid Cancer, NSCLC, and Other Advanced Solid Tumors
L664: ## Trial Design
L665: ## The Applicant's Description:
L666: Study BLU-667-1101 is a Phase 1/2, open-label, FIH study designed to evaluate the safety, tolerability, PK, pharmacodynamics, and antineoplastic activity of pralsetinib in patients with advanced, unresectable, RET-altered NSCLC, MTC, and other RET-altered solid tumors. The study included a Phase 1 dose escalation part to determine the MTD and RP2D of pralsetinib, followed by a Phase 2 expansion part to assess the clinical efficacy of pralsetinib in specific tumor types and treatment settings (measured primarily by ORR), and further define the safety and tolerability at the RP2D. Phase 1 ran until the RP2D was determined, at which point Phase 2 was initiated and is still ongoing. An overview of the study design is available in Figure 3.
L667: **Figure 3: BLU-667-1101 Study Design**

Safety results are presented for all patients treated with the RP2D (pooled) as starting dose (regardless of study phase) as of the data cutoff of 18 November 2019.
L683: ## **Trial Location**
L684: In Phase 1, patients were enrolled in 6 centers across North America and Europe. In Phase 2, patients were enrolled in 53 centers across North America, Europe, and Asia.
L685: ## **Choice of Control Group**
L686: There is no currently accepted standard of care for RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients.
L687: ## Diagnostic Criteria
L688: Patients with confirmed oncogenic RET fusion as determined by local testing of tumor or ctDNA in blood were enrolled.
L689: ## Key Inclusion Criteria
L690: ## Phase 1
L691: - Men or women \ge 18 years with pathologically documented, definitively diagnosed unresectable advanced solid tumor;
- Progressed to standard therapy or not adequately responded/intolerant to standard therapy or Investigator determined standard therapy to be inappropriate or there was no accepted standard therapy;
- ECOG performance status of 0 to 1.
L692: ## Phase 2 – RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients
L693: - Group 1: Men or women \ge 18 years with unresectable, pathologically documented, definitively diagnosed locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC with a RET fusion previously treated with a platinum-based chemotherapy;
- Group 2: Men or women \ge 18 years with unresectable, pathologically documented, definitively diagnosed locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC with a RET fusion not previously treated with a platinum-based chemotherapy, including those who have not had any systemic therapy. Up until Amendment 9, naïve patients were only allowed if the Investigator had determined that treatment with standard therapy was not appropriate. With Amendment 9, prior platinum chemotherapy in the neoadjuvant and adjuvant setting was permitted if the last dose of platinum treatment was \ge 4 months before the first dose of pralsetinib (introduced after cutoff).
- Both groups:Had an oncogenic RET fusion, as determined by local testing of tumor or ctDNA in blood;
- Had measurable disease as per RECIST v1.1;
- ECOG performance status of 0 to 1.
L694: ## Key Exclusion Criteria
L695: - Known primary driver alteration other than RET;
- Medical history of myocardial infarction or unstable angina within the previous 6 months; clinically significant, uncontrolled cardiovascular disease (including congestive heart failure NYHA Grade III or IV); uncontrolled hypertension; or clinically significant, uncontrolled arrhythmias;
- Previous treatment with selective RET inhibitor (except Group 6); any systemic anticancer therapy and all forms of radiotherapy within 14 days or 5 half-lives before first dose of pralsetinib; immunotherapy or other antibody therapy within 28 days before first dose of
L696: pralsetinib; or neutrophil growth factor support within 14 days of the first dose of pralsetinib;

- Inadequate hematology and clinical chemistry values within 14 days before first dose of pralsetinib;
- QTcF &gt; 470 ms and history of prolonged QT syndrome or Torsades de pointes;
- Presence of CNS metastases or a primary CNS tumor that is associated with progressive neurological symptoms or requires increasing doses of corticosteroids to control the CNS disease;
- Presence of clinically symptomatic interstitial lung disease or interstitial pneumonitis, including radiation pneumonitis;
- Pregnancy and breastfeeding.
L697: ## Dose Selection
L698: Initial dose level chosen to be studied for Phase 1 was based on nonclinical data from 28-day repeated-dose toxicology studies conducted in rats and monkeys. Based on the overall data from Phase 1, a pralsetinib dose of 400 mg QD was determined to be the recommended starting dose for Phase 2.
L699: ## Study Treatments, Treatment Assignment, and Blinding
L700: As this was an open-label study, there was no randomization or blinding. Pralsetinib was administered continuously daily without interruption (unless due to interruption or discontinuation due to toxicity, progression, or other reasons).
L701: In Phase 1, pralsetinib was administered once daily (30 to 600 mg) or twice daily (100/100 mg or 200/100 mg) to determine the MTD and RP2D. Patients were assigned to a dose cohort according to a dose escalation scheme prespecified in the protocol. In Phase 2, pralsetinib was administered at RP2D (400 mg QD).
L702: ## Dose Modification, Dose Discontinuation
L703: Management guidance and dose modifications during both phases were provided in the protocol.
L704: In Phase 1, intra-patient dose escalation and dose modification for AEs were allowed. To minimize the number of patients treated at potentially inactive doses, intra-patient dose escalation was permitted after a patient had completed \ge 2 cycles of treatment without experiencing Grade 3/4 AEs related to pralsetinib or a DLT. Patients who experienced a DLT interrupted administration of pralsetinib and were followed until the DLT resolved to Grade \le 1 or until the patient's baseline value was achieved, if higher. After resolution of the AE, with a maximum of 2 weeks' dose interruption, the patient could resume therapy with a reduction of 1 dose level. A maximum of 3 dose reductions were permitted for any patient.
L705: In Phase 2, dose modification for AEs, ADRs, and dose re-escalation after resolution of ADRs were allowed. For AEs, pralsetinib dose reductions to &lt; 100 mg QD were not permitted. If a patient required dose reduction &lt; 100 mg QD, study treatment was discontinued.
L706: ## Administrative Structure
L707: Safety was monitored on a regular basis, with the Sponsor Clinical Study Team hosting regular teleconferences with Investigators. In addition, the Sponsor's Safety Management Team reviewed and managed cumulative safety data at periodic intervals (approximately quarterly) and ad hoc, as needed.
L708: During Phase 1, the Sponsor's Clinical Study Team and the Investigators met at the end of each dose cohort to review all data. Dose escalation decisions were based on all relevant, available data, and not solely on DLT information.
L709: During Phase 2, all available data were reviewed approximately every 1 to 2 months at a safety review meeting that included the Investigators and the Sponsor's Clinical Study Team. After Protocol Amendment 9, a Safety Review Committee was introduced in light of the growing enrollment size. The Independent Data Monitoring Committee (3 members independent of the study) replaced the Safety Review Committee in December 2019. A BICR assessment was instated to assess tumor scans.
